154 words; within budget

The T5 results show why acceptance alone is an incomplete predictor of latency. In English-German translation with an 11B-parameter T5-XXL target, T5-small and T5-large achieve 2.6-fold and 1.4-fold speedups over ordinary autoregressive decoding under standard sampling at batch size one on a single TPU-v4. Acceptance rises from 0.62 to 0.71 with the larger proposal, yet higher overlap does not make it faster. The runs also use seven and three guesses, respectively, so they compare complete decoding configurations rather than isolate proposal size. T5-small performs better under greedy decoding, reaching 0.75 acceptance and a 3.4-fold speedup. Its summarization gains are lower, at 2.3-fold under standard sampling and 3.1-fold under greedy decoding. Together, these comparisons support selecting proposals by useful tokens per unit time rather than distributional overlap alone. Faster generation need not mean less arithmetic: rejected guesses waste computation, and acceleration requires spare capacity for concurrent verification.

P03 / Abstract / Sample 3

FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

171 words; within budget

Avoiding quadratic attention storage does not by itself keep a GPU busy: normalization arithmetic and poorly partitioned work can still limit exact attention. FlashAttention-2 reorganizes tiled attention around independently normalized query rows. Assigning these rows to separate warps removes forward inter-warp reductions, while distributing row blocks across thread blocks exposes parallelism when batches or head counts are small. Delaying output normalization reduces non-matrix arithmetic without approximating softmax or losing linear auxiliary memory. On an A100 80GB GPU, attention benchmarks with sequences of 512 to 16k tokens and 16k total tokens show 1.7-3.0-fold speedups over FlashAttention. For 1.3B- and 2.7B-parameter GPT-style models trained on eight A100 GPUs at 2k or 8k context, gains reach 1.3-fold over FlashAttention, with peak reported throughput of 225 TFLOPs/s per GPU. Multi-query decoding has a different bottleneck: loading the key-value cache. Spreading these loads across thread blocks yields an attention-kernel speedup of up to sevenfold over FasterTransformer on A100. The training comparison shows that kernel acceleration delivers smaller, context-dependent whole-model gains.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: FlashAttention-2 assigns query rows to warps and blocks and delays normalization, reducing reductions and non-matrix arithmetic while preserving exact attention and linear auxiliary memory. Multi-query decoding distributes KV-cache loads, addressing a different bottleneck.

Scope: A100 80GB attention tests, 512-16k sequences with 16k total tokens, gain 1.7-3.0x over FlashAttention. Eight-A100 1.3B/2.7B GPT-style training at 2k/8k gains up to 1.3x, showing smaller, context-dependent benefits. Multi-query kernels gain up to 7x over FasterTransformer on A100.

Witness: kernel acceleration delivers smaller, context-dependent whole-model gains

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P03 Sections 3.1-3.3 support independently normalized rows, deferred normalization, forward reduction removal, and decoding's KV-loading partition. Reading recovers these mechanisms and exactness/linear storage. Sections 4.1-4.3, Table 1, and Figure 5 support quoted shapes, kernel gains, training peak, and sevenfold multi-query kernel gain. Reading's 'smaller, context-dependent benefits' preserves the prose's separation of kernel and whole-model evidence; its hardware and model/context limits adequately bound the chosen relationship.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P03 Sections 3.1-3.3 establish delayed normalization, independent query-row scheduling, exactness, and linear auxiliary memory. Section 4.1 gives the A100 sequence/token controls; Table 1 supports smaller, context-dependent eight-GPU training gains and the 225 TFLOPs/s peak. Sections 3.2 and 4.2 establish the separate decoding cache-loading bottleneck and FasterTransformer kernel comparison. The reader faithfully separates those mechanisms and measurement scopes.

P03 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

145 words; within budget

Each query row has its own softmax denominator, so different rows can be assigned to different warps without splitting the calculation of a single output row. FlashAttention-2 uses this independence: every warp accesses the key and value tiles but produces only its assigned query rows. Unlike splitting keys among warps, this arrangement removes shared-memory reductions and synchronization from the forward output calculation. Query-row blocks also run independently across thread blocks, supplying parallel work when batch size and head count are too small to occupy the GPU. Within a row block, online softmax maintains the running maximum, exponential sum, and unnormalized weighted output. A changed maximum rescales accumulated quantities, but division by the sum occurs only after the final key tile, reducing non-matrix arithmetic while preserving exact attention. Rowwise log-sum-exp values support probability recomputation during backpropagation instead of quadratic storage; the backward pass still requires synchronization.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Assigning independent query rows to warps lets each warp share key/value tiles while producing its own rows, removing forward shared-memory reductions and synchronization. Independent row blocks increase GPU occupancy; deferred online-softmax normalization reduces non-matrix arithmetic without changing exact attention.

Scope: Block parallelism helps when batches/head counts are small. Changed row maxima require rescaling; saved rowwise log-sum-exp replaces quadratic probability storage through backward recomputation, which still requires synchronization.

Witness: the backward pass still requires synchronization

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

135 words; within budget

FlashAttention's split-key layout gives each warp only a partial contribution to every output row, requiring shared-memory writes, synchronization, and summation. FlashAttention-2 instead assigns each warp its own query rows while making key and value tiles available to all warps. Since softmax couples scores within a row rather than across rows, each warp can compute complete outputs for its assigned rows without an inter-warp reduction. The same independence permits separate thread blocks to process query-row blocks, increasing occupancy when batches or head counts are small. Within a block, the algorithm tracks a running maximum and exponential sum, rescales an unnormalized output when the maximum changes, and divides only after the last key tile. Shared initial draft

FlashAttention-2 assigns different query rows to different warps while making each key and value tile available to all of them. Because attention normalizes each query row separately, a warp can compute its rows of scores and their value-weighted outputs without summing partial outputs produced by other warps. The earlier split-key arrangement instead required shared-memory writes, synchronization, and reductions. Independent query-row blocks also run on separate thread blocks, increasing available parallel work when a small batch or few heads would otherwise leave GPU multiprocessors idle. Within each row block, online softmax accumulates an unnormalized output, rescales it when the running maximum changes, and applies the accumulated denominator only after the final key tile. This reduces normalization work while retaining exact softmax attention. Saving rowwise log-sum-exp permits probabilities to be recomputed during backpropagation rather than storing the quadratic attention matrix.

P03 / Results / Sample 3

FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

154 words; within budget

Longer context makes the training benefit of faster attention more pronounced, but it does not turn a kernel speedup into an equal whole-model gain. On eight A100 80GB GPUs, replacing FlashAttention with FlashAttention-2 raises reported throughput for a 1.3B-parameter GPT-style model from 189 to 196 TFLOPs/s per GPU at 2k context, compared with 170 to 220 at 8k. The 2.7B model reaches 225 TFLOPs/s per GPU at 8k, versus 175 with FlashAttention. These comparisons place the largest training gain near 1.3-fold, below the 1.7-3.0-fold attention-kernel gains in the separate A100 benchmarks. Those benchmarks also expose the storage constraint: standard PyTorch attention runs out of memory at 16k, whereas tiled implementations complete that setting. The reported peak 72% model FLOPs utilization counts causal attention using the conventional unhalved training formula. It should therefore be interpreted using that accounting convention, rather than as a direct count of executed arithmetic.

P04 / Abstract / Sample 3

Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

176 words; within budget

Autoregressive generation can leave computational capacity unused while a large model waits for each preceding token. Speculative decoding uses that capacity to verify a cheaper model's proposed continuation in parallel, without requiring the draft to reproduce the target's probabilities. An acceptance rule preserves the probability mass shared by the models, and a residual distribution corrects rejected proposals, yielding exactly the target sampling distribution for any draft model without retraining the target. The analysis connects latency to the balance between acceptance and proposal cost, assuming independent, identically distributed acceptance rates, sufficiently long generations, and concurrent verification that takes no longer than one target step. In batch-one tests on a single TPU-v4 against T5X decoding, a 77M-parameter T5-small draft accelerates 11B-parameter T5-XXL by 2.6-fold on English-German translation and 2.3-fold on summarization under standard sampling. Greedy decoding reaches 3.4-fold and 3.1-fold, respectively. Larger draft models achieve higher acceptance but lower walltime gains, showing that improved agreement must compensate for proposal cost. These gains exploit concurrency rather than fewer arithmetic operations and require spare computational resources.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Parallel verification of cheap drafts, probability-mass acceptance, and residual correction preserve exact target sampling without retraining. Latency improves through concurrency, not fewer arithmetic operations; greater draft agreement must offset proposal cost.

Scope: Any draft is valid. Speed analysis assumes i.i.d. acceptance, long generations, and verification costing one target step. Batch-one 77M/11B T5 tests on one TPU-v4 versus T5X yield translation/summarization speedups of 2.6x/2.3x sampling and 3.4x/3.1x greedy. Spare compute is required.

Witness: Larger draft models achieve higher acceptance but lower walltime gains

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Reading explicitly retains Section 3.3's i.i.d., long-generation, unchanged verification-latency assumptions and Section 3.4/selected limitations' concurrency-versus-arithmetic and spare-compute boundary. These conditions adequately delimit the selected exactness and latency claims.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. The reading faithfully reconstructs that ordering and the theoretical and empirical boundaries rather than treating agreement alone as sufficient.

Autoregressive decoding repeatedly invokes a large model even when available hardware could evaluate several candidate continuations concurrently. P04 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

Fast Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

152 words; within budget

Let p and q denote target and draft token distributions after sampling adjustments. A token x drawn from q is accepted with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)); after rejection, a replacement is drawn from the normalized positive part of p - q. Acceptance supplies mass min(p(x), q(x)), and the correction supplies the missing target mass, so the combined distribution is exactly p. Parallel verification evaluates every draft prefix, but only the proposals preceding the first rejection are retained. Correction at that prefix, or an extra target token if all proposals pass, guarantees at least one output per iteration. For gamma proposals and mean acceptance alpha, independent, identically distributed acceptance rates give an expected yield of (1 - alpha^(gamma + 1))/(1 - alpha). Dividing this yield by 1 + gamma*c predicts walltime speedup for sufficiently long outputs if concurrent verification costs one target step; c is the draft-to-target step-time ratio.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Probability-ratio acceptance contributes shared draft/target mass; residual resampling after rejection supplies missing target mass, exactly recovering p. Parallel verification retains proposals only before the first rejection, then emits a correction, or an extra target token after full acceptance, guaranteeing progress.

Scope: Distributions include sampling adjustments. Under i.i.d. acceptance mean alpha, yield is (1-alpha^(gamma+1))/(1-alpha); dividing by 1+gamma*c predicts speedup for long outputs when concurrent verification costs one target step. gamma counts proposals; c is relative draft latency.

Witness: the combined distribution is exactly p

Reading recovers sampling adjustment, i.i.d. acceptance, gamma as proposal count, c as step-time ratio, sufficiently long outputs, and concurrency costing one target step. Those explicit conditions adequately bound the analytical relationship.

Section 3.1 gives the stated i.i.d. yield; Section 3.3/Theorem 3.8 supplies the walltime denominator and long-output/concurrent-latency qualifications. The prose defines gamma and c and distinguishes the correction mechanism from the timing model. The reader accurately retains those relations and conditions.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

147 words; within budget

Speculative sampling preserves the target distribution without requiring perfect draft agreement. At a fixed prefix, p and q denote target and draft distributions after applying the desired sampling rule. A token x drawn from q is accepted with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)), contributing mass min(p(x), q(x)). Rejected draws are replaced using the normalized positive part of p - q; weighted by rejection probability, this supplies the remaining target mass. The total is therefore p, regardless of draft quality. The decoder checks prefixes of a gamma-token draft in parallel, retains proposals before the first rejection, and corrects that position. If all pass, it adds a target-sampled token. Each step yields one to gamma + 1 tokens; under independent, identically distributed acceptance rates with mean alpha, the expected yield is (1 - alpha^(gamma + 1))/(1 - alpha), measuring reduced serial target calls rather than reduced computation.

Shared initial draft

At a given prefix, let p and q be the target and draft token distributions after applying the desired sampling rule. A draft token x is accepted with probability min(1, p(x)/q(x)). If rejected, its replacement is sampled from the normalized positive part of p - q. Accepted proposals contribute probability mass min(p(x), q(x)); the rejection branch supplies exactly the missing target mass, so their sum is p(x). The decoder checks draft prefixes in parallel, stops acceptance at the first rejection, and applies this correction there. If all proposals pass, it samples an extra target token. Each iteration therefore produces between one and gamma + 1 tokens. With independent, identically distributed acceptance rates of mean alpha, the expected token count is (1 - alpha^(gamma + 1))/(1 - alpha). This yield measures reduced serial target calls, not necessarily reduced computation.

These measured latency gains are specific to the implemented T5 experiments; GPT-like and LaMDA tests report acceptance rather than walltime. Rejection discards already evaluated continuations, so the method can consume more arithmetic work even while finishing sooner on hardware with spare compute.

P05 / Abstract / Sample 1

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

176 words; within budget

LoRA reduces the parameter cost of adaptation, but its weight updates can couple changes that full fine-tuning adjusts more independently. In a VL-BART case study, columnwise weight decomposition reveals positively correlated magnitude and direction changes for LoRA, compared with a negative correlation for full fine-tuning. Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation (DoRA) responds by learning column magnitudes separately and applying low-rank updates to normalized directions. Its weights can be merged before inference without additional latency. Using the reference LoRA configuration with an adjusted learning rate, LLaMA-7B DoRA reaches 78.4% average accuracy across eight commonsense reasoning tasks with 0.84% trainable parameters, versus the published baseline's 74.7% with 0.83%. A half-rank variant reaches 77.5% with 0.43%. Multimodal evaluations also favor DoRA's aggregate scores over LoRA's, although the VL-BART video-task average remains below full fine-tuning. In a separate ablation, detaching the normalization denominator reduces LLaMA-7B training memory by 24.4% with a 0.2-percentage-point accuracy decrease. These results support reallocating adaptation capacity between magnitude and direction instead of relying solely on a larger low-rank parameter budget.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

P05 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

125 words; within budget

DoRA separates each weight column's length from its direction so that the low-rank factors need not also encode the desired scale change. It freezes the pretrained matrix W0, adds a trainable low-rank product BA, and normalizes the resulting columns before multiplying them by independently learned magnitudes. Initial magnitudes equal the pretrained column norms, and BA starts at zero, preserving the original weights at initialization. Without detachment, differentiating this normalization rescales the weight gradient and removes its component parallel to the current direction. The evaluated implementation recomputes the column norms in each forward pass but treats them as constants during backpropagation to reduce memory use. This approximation preserves the forward decomposition and independent magnitude learning while replacing the projected directional gradient with a scaled weight gradient.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: DoRA normalizes frozen W0 plus trainable BA and applies independent column magnitudes, separating direction from length so factors need not encode scale. Treating recomputed norms as constants in backpropagation saves memory but changes exact projected directional gradients to scaled weight gradients.

Scope: Pretrained column norms initialize magnitudes; BA starts at zero, preserving initial weights. Without detachment, differentiation removes the direction-parallel gradient component. The evaluated approximation preserves forward decomposition and independent magnitude learning.

Witness: replacing the projected directional gradient with a scaled weight gradient

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Eq. 6 supports removing the parallel gradient component before detachment; Sec. 4.3/Eq. 11 supports the implemented scaled gradient with norms recomputed as constants for backpropagation. Prose explicitly calls this an approximation preserving only the forward decomposition and magnitude learning. Reading recovers that adequate representation/derivative boundary and the initialization relation.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P05 Equation 5 and initialization discussion support frozen W0, trainable BA, independently learned magnitudes, pretrained norms, and zero updates. Equation 6 supports removing the direction-parallel gradient component. Section 4.3 and Equation 11 support recomputed norms treated as constants, unchanged magnitude gradients, and scaling alone. The reader preserves the prose's forward decomposition and exact-versus-implemented backward distinction.

149 words; within budget

An additive low-rank update must express both directional and scale changes in pretrained weight columns. DoRA gives rescaling its own parameter: each adapted column is a learned magnitude multiplied by the unit direction of W0 + BA, where W0 is frozen and A and B are trainable low-rank factors. Initial magnitudes equal the pretrained column norms, and BA starts at zero, preserving the original weights at initialization. Column normalization removes scale from the quantity controlled by the low-rank factors, allowing the magnitude vector to adjust it separately. Differentiating through normalization rescales the weight gradient and removes its component parallel to the current direction. The evaluated implementation instead recomputes the denominator in each forward pass but detaches it during backpropagation. This reduces the gradient graph's memory cost and retains magnitude learning, while using a scaled rather than projected directional gradient. The distinction separates the analyzed optimization geometry from the memory-saving implementation.

Shared initial draft

DoRA represents each adapted weight column as a learned scalar magnitude times a unit direction. Starting from the pretrained matrix W0, it computes the direction by normalizing W0 + BA columnwise, where A and B are trainable low-rank factors, and learns the magnitude vector separately. Initial magnitudes equal the pretrained column norms and the initial low-rank product is zero, so adaptation begins at the original weights. This arrangement lets the low-rank factors concentrate on direction instead of jointly expressing changes in direction and scale. The full normalization derivative rescales the weight gradient and removes its component parallel to the current direction. In the evaluated implementation, however, the denominator is detached during backpropagation while still being recomputed in the forward pass. This memory-saving modification retains independent magnitude learning but replaces the projected directional gradient with a scaled weight gradient.

P05 / Results / Sample 1

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

162 words; within budget

Half-rank DoRA improves average commonsense accuracy while roughly halving LoRA's trainable parameter budget. Across eight tasks, LLaMA-7B reaches 77.5% with 0.43% trainable parameters, versus the published LoRA baseline's 74.7% with 0.83%. The LLaMA-13B gain is smaller: 80.8% versus 80.5%, using 0.35% versus 0.67% trainable parameters. Because DoRA also adjusts the reference learning rate, these comparisons support better performance at smaller parameter budgets without isolating decomposition as its cause. The VL-BART query-weight analysis tests a complementary claim about update behavior: magnitude-direction correlations are -0.31 for DoRA, -0.62 for full fine-tuning, and 0.83 for LoRA. Resembling full fine-tuning's update pattern does not ensure matching its performance. On VL-BART video tasks, DoRA raises the aggregate from LoRA's 83.5 to 85.4 but remains below full fine-tuning's 87.5, with the largest shortfall on YC2C captioning. Visual instruction tuning with LLaVA-1.5-7B likewise improves the average while scoring below LoRA on VQAv2 and VQAT.

Scope: Adjusted learning rates prevent attributing gains solely to decomposition. Video aggregate improves 83.5 to 85.4 but trails full fine-tuning's 87.5, especially YC2C. LLaVA's average improves despite VQAv2/VQAT regressions versus LoRA.

P06 / Abstract / Sample 1

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

173 words; within budget

GaLore reduces the memory cost of language-model training by compressing optimizer statistics rather than constraining weights to a low-rank factorization. It projects gradients into a compact subspace, applies the optimizer there, and maps updates back to the full weight matrix. Refreshing that subspace allows successive updates to accumulate beyond a fixed low-rank representation. Analysis of structured gradients supports conditional rank reduction and fixed-subspace convergence, rather than a general guarantee for periodically refreshed Adam training. In C4 pretraining, a single untuned LLaMA-7B trial over 19.7 billion tokens on 64 A100 GPUs yields validation perplexities of 14.65 for 8-bit GaLore and 14.61 for 8-bit Adam. Separately, projection combined with 8-bit optimizer states and per-layer updates reduces measured 7B training memory to 22.0 GB at a small per-device token batch, without checkpointing or offloading. GLUE fine-tuning remains close to full fine-tuning, with small aggregate gains over LoRA. The 1B implementation has lower measured throughput than 8-bit Adam, so the contribution is reduced memory demand with largely retained model quality, not faster training.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: GaLore compresses optimizer statistics through projected gradients while retaining full weights; refreshing projections permits changes beyond a fixed low-rank representation. This reduces memory while largely retaining model quality.

Scope: One untuned 7B C4 trial on 64 A100s gives perplexity 14.65 versus 14.61 after 19.7B tokens. Separate 22.0 GB profiling combines projection, 8-bit states, per-layer updates, small batches, no checkpointing/offloading. GLUE stays close to full fine-tuning. Theory is conditional and fixed-subspace; measured 1B throughput is lower.

Witness: not faster training

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Table 4 supports small aggregate GLUE gains. Prose's 'Separately' and 'not faster training' supply adequate experimental boundaries, which the reading recovers alongside the mechanism and conditional theory.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P06 Sections 4.1-4.3 support projected optimizer statistics, dense-weight updates, refreshed subspaces, and combined memory-saving techniques. Sections 5.3-5.5 and Table 4 support the separate 7B quality/profile results, near-full-tuning GLUE averages, and slower 1B throughput. The prose's 'Separately' and 'not faster training' boundaries are retained in both reader fields; its conditional-theory account does not claim refreshed-Adam convergence.

P06 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

134 words; within budget

For a weight matrix W with m <= n, GaLore uses the gradient's leading r left singular vectors as a projector P, compresses G to P^T G, and updates Adam's moments in this r-by-n space. Multiplying the processed update by P maps it back to W; the weights themselves remain full dimensional. This moves memory-intensive optimizer statistics into a smaller representation without requiring a fixed low-rank weight factorization. Periodically recomputing P lets successive updates explore different subspaces when useful gradient directions change during pretraining. The fixed-projection theorem gives a conditional contraction bound for the projected gradient, assuming structured Lipschitz gradient components, bounded weights, no adaptive gradient regularization, and sufficient projected curvature relative to component variation. Its guarantee concerns progress within one subspace, not convergence of the periodically refreshed Adam implementation to a full-space optimum.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: For m <= n; refreshing P follows changing gradient directions. The theorem assumes fixed projections, structured Lipschitz components, bounded weights, no adaptive regularization, and sufficient projected curvature relative to variation. It guarantees conditional subspace progress, not refreshed Adam convergence to a full-space optimum.

Witness: Its guarantee concerns progress within one subspace

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Theorem 3.8/Eq. 11 support the stated structured continuity, bounded-weight, fixed-projection, nonadaptive and curvature restrictions. Prose's 'progress within one subspace' explicitly limits the guarantee; reading recovers that scope and does not infer a full-space optimum for refreshed Adam.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P06 Algorithm 2 and Section 4.2 support leading left singular vectors, P^T G, r-by-n moments, and lifting onto full-dimensional weights. Section 4.1 supports periodic subspace changes. Theorem 3.8 supplies the stated fixed-projection, continuity, weight-bound, regularization, and curvature limitations. The reader recovers both the compression operation and the prose's restriction to conditional subspace progress rather than a refreshed-Adam full-space convergence guarantee.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

154 words; within budget

GaLore compresses the optimizer's representation of an update while leaving the weight matrix itself full-sized. For an m-by-n matrix with m <= n, the leading r left singular vectors of the current gradient form a projector P. The compressed gradient P^T G has r-by-n dimensions, so Adam stores its two moment arrays in that smaller space. The processed update is scaled, multiplied by P, and added directly to the original weights. Periodic singular-value decomposition refreshes P, allowing successive updates to leave the initial subspace rather than enforcing a fixed low-rank weight factorization. The supporting theorem concerns a narrower operation: with fixed projections, structured Lipschitz gradient components, bounded weights, no adaptive regularization, and sufficient projected curvature relative to component variation, the projected gradient contracts toward zero. This guarantees progress within the selected subspace, not convergence of periodically refreshed Adam to a full-space optimum. The refresh rule addresses subspace coverage empirically rather than inheriting that guarantee.

Shared initial draft

The key operation is to move optimizer state, not model weights, into a lower-dimensional representation. For a weight matrix W with m rows and n columns, where m <= n, GaLore takes the leading r left singular vectors of the gradient as a projector P and forms the compressed gradient P^T G. Adam's moments are stored and updated in this r-by-n space; the processed update is multiplied by P and added directly to W. Refreshing P periodically allows accumulated weight changes to span more than one fixed subspace. This distinction matters because a high-rank optimum need not admit a fixed low-rank parameterization. The fixed-projection theorem establishes contraction of the projected gradient for structured, Lipschitz gradient components, bounded weights, no adaptive regularization, and a sufficient curvature margin over their variation. It does not establish convergence of the periodically refreshed Adam implementation to a full-space optimum.

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

167 words; within budget

In 1B C4 pretraining, GaLore reaches validation perplexity of 15.64 versus full-rank Adam's 15.56, with estimated memory for weights and optimizer states of 4.38 versus 7.80 GB. LoRA and ReLoRA require 6.17 GB each but reach perplexities of 19.21 and 18.33. With matched low ranks and equal learning-rate search budgets, this favors gradient-state compression over the evaluated adapter approaches; ReLoRA receives no full-rank warmup. These memory estimates exclude gradients and activations. At 7B, one untuned trial on 64 A100 GPUs reaches 14.65 perplexity versus 14.61 for 8-bit Adam. The separate 22.0 GB footprint measurement establishes that a small token batch fits a 24 GB device, not that this run used one consumer GPU. Memory savings also carry a throughput cost: at a 256-token batch in the matching 1B measurement configuration, 8-bit GaLore processes 1019.63 tokens per second versus 1205.31 for 8-bit Adam. The benefit is lower memory demand with near-baseline perplexity, rather than faster training.

P05 / Abstract / Sample 2

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

179 words; within budget

Low-rank adaptation saves parameters, yet the same factors must adjust both the lengths and directions of pretrained weight columns. A VL-BART checkpoint analysis finds these changes positively correlated under LoRA but negatively correlated under full fine-tuning, motivating Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation (DoRA). DoRA learns a separate column magnitude vector and applies low-rank updates to normalized directions, making column length adjustable without a dense trainable matrix. The adapted weights can be merged before inference. Across eight commonsense benchmarks with pooled training data, LLaMA-7B achieves 78.4% mean accuracy versus 74.7% for a LoRA baseline reported in prior work, using trainable parameter fractions of 0.84% and 0.83%, respectively. DoRA follows the baseline configuration but adjusts the learning rate; halving its rank yields 77.5% accuracy with 0.43% trainable parameters. Image- and video-text scores also improve over reported LoRA baselines, although the video aggregate remains below full fine-tuning. Detaching the normalization denominator lowers training memory relative to unmodified DoRA with small score changes. These comparisons support separating magnitude learning from low-rank directional adaptation to improve the tested accuracy-parameter tradeoff.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: Eight pooled-training LLaMA-7B benchmarks: 78.4% versus published LoRA's 74.7%, with 0.84% versus 0.83% trainable parameters and adjusted learning rate. Half-rank yields 77.5% with 0.43%. Multimodal scores improve, but video trails full fine-tuning. Denominator detachment reduces memory with small score changes.

Witness: improve the tested accuracy-parameter tradeoff

P05 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

145 words; within budget

DoRA separates two jobs that an ordinary low-rank update performs together: changing a weight column's length and changing its direction. It constructs each adapted column from the corresponding column of W0 + BA, divides by that column's norm, and multiplies by a learned scalar. The pretrained matrix W0 stays frozen; the magnitude scalars start at its column norms, and zero initialization of B preserves the original weights before training. Normalization therefore lets the small magnitude vector control column lengths while the trainable low-rank factors alter directions. In the exact derivative, the gradient with respect to the unnormalized direction is scaled and projected perpendicular to the current direction. The experiments detach the denominator to reduce backpropagation memory: its value tracks the updated factors, but its derivative is omitted. This keeps the forward separation of magnitude and direction while replacing the projected directional gradient with a scaled gradient.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: DoRA normalizes columns of frozen W0 plus BA and rescales them with learned magnitudes, assigning column lengths to magnitudes and directions to low-rank factors.

Scope: Magnitudes start at pretrained norms; zero B preserves initial weights. Experiments recompute but detach normalization denominators, reducing memory while omitting their derivatives: forward separation remains, but scaled gradients replace the exact scaled, perpendicular projections.

Witness: replacing the projected directional gradient with a scaled gradient

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P05 Eq. 5/Sec. 4.1 support columnwise normalization, learned magnitudes, frozen W0 and initialization. Eqs. 6/11 and Sec. 4.3 support the exact scaled perpendicular gradient versus recomputed, detached denominator used in experiments. Prose's 'keeps the forward separation' but replaces the projected gradient states the necessary boundary between representation and backward computation. The reading recovers this mechanism and boundary directly; no benchmark comparison is required for this selected methods function.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P05 Equation 5 supports separate column magnitudes and normalized W0+BA; its initialization discussion supports frozen W0, pretrained norms, and zero B. Equation 6 and Section 4.3 distinguish exact scaled perpendicular projection from the evaluated detached-denominator gradient in Equation 11. The reader reconstructs the mechanism centrally and places the implemented derivative boundary in scope; neither field claims the exact projection survives detachment.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

140 words; within budget

A low-rank additive update normally determines both the length and direction of each weight column. DoRA separates these roles: each column is a learned magnitude multiplying the normalized column of W0 + BA, where W0 is frozen and A and B are trainable low-rank factors. Magnitudes start at the pretrained column norms, and initializing B to zero preserves the original weights before adaptation. Normalization prevents the factors from directly setting column length, leaving that control to a small magnitude vector rather than a dense trainable matrix. Exact differentiation projects directional gradients orthogonally to the current column direction. The evaluated implementation instead detaches the normalization denominator during backpropagation to reduce memory. It still recomputes the norm as the factors change, preserving the forward parameterization, but omits the norm derivative. Directional learning therefore uses a scaled gradient rather than inheriting the exact projection.

Shared initial draft

DoRA represents each adapted weight column by a learned magnitude multiplying a normalized direction. Starting from the pretrained matrix W0, it sets the magnitudes to the original column norms and constructs directions from W0 + BA, where A and B are trainable low-rank factors and W0 remains frozen. Initializing B to zero preserves the pretrained weights at the start of fine-tuning. The normalization prevents directional-factor updates from directly setting column length, so the small magnitude vector supplies a separate degree of freedom without requiring a dense trainable matrix. Differentiating through this normalization projects directional gradients orthogonally to the current column direction. The experiments instead detach the denominator during backpropagation to reduce memory: the norm is recomputed during training, but its derivative is omitted. This approximation retains the forward parameterization while replacing the projected gradient with a scaled gradient.

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

162 words; within budget

DoRA's advantage in the LLaMA-7B rank sweep is not simply a consequence of training more parameters. Across eight commonsense tests, rank-8 DoRA averages 77.9% accuracy against LoRA's 40.7%, with trainable fractions of 0.22% and 0.21%. It also exceeds rank-32 LoRA's 74.7%, supporting a better accuracy-parameter tradeoff under the reported recipes, although learning rates can differ. Multimodal results further distinguish improvement over LoRA from recovery of full fine-tuning performance. P06 / Abstract / Sample 2

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

178 words; within budget

Optimizer-state compression need not require a low-rank parameterization of the weights. GaLore projects gradients into a compact subspace, maintains optimizer statistics there, and expands each update onto dense weights. Periodically refreshing the projection lets accumulated updates move beyond one fixed subspace. The analysis bounds gradient stable rank under specified forms and conditions and establishes conditional contraction of the projected gradient with fixed projections and an identity regularizer; these guarantees do not cover the refreshed Adam implementation. In C4 pretraining, a 1B model reaches validation perplexity 15.64, versus 15.56 for full-rank Adam and 19.21 for rank-matched LoRA with frozen initial weights, with equal learning-rate search budgets. A separate single-trial 7B run reaches 14.65 versus 14.61 for 8-bit Adam after 19.7 billion tokens on 64 A100 GPUs. Single-device profiling combines projection, 8-bit optimization, and per-layer updates to fit 7B training within 22.0 GB at small token batches, without offloading or checkpointing. P06 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

146 words; within budget

GaLore reduces Adam's storage by compressing gradient statistics while keeping the weights dense. For a gradient G of an m-by-n matrix with m <= n, it uses the leading r left singular vectors as a projection P and forms R = P^T G. Adam stores its two moment arrays in this r-by-n space; the resulting update is scaled and multiplied by P before modifying the weights. Recomputing P periodically lets successive updates occupy different subspaces, so their sum is not restricted to one rank-r factorization. The theorem for fixed projections and an identity regularizer establishes conditional contraction only of the projected gradient, not convergence of this refreshed Adam procedure. The refresh therefore serves an operational purpose that the theorem cannot ensure: following useful directions as the gradient subspace changes. Rare refreshes can retain an unhelpful subspace; frequent ones add decomposition cost and may disrupt accumulated optimizer statistics.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: GaLore projects gradients using leading left singular vectors, stores Adam moments in r-by-n space, then scales and maps updates back to dense weights. Periodic projection refresh permits accumulated updates beyond a fixed rank-r factorization.

Scope: For m-by-n matrices with m <= n. Fixed-projection, identity-regularizer theory establishes conditional projected-gradient contraction, not refreshed Adam convergence. Rare refreshes may retain unhelpful directions; frequent refreshes cost decompositions and may disrupt optimizer statistics.

Witness: their sum is not restricted to one rank-r factorization

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P06 Sec. 4.2/Algorithm 2 support left-sided projection, compact moments and scaled lifting; P^T G has r-by-n dimensions. Sec. 4.1 supports refresh and its cost/statistics tradeoff. Theorem 3.8 concerns conditional projected-gradient contraction with fixed projections and identity regularization. Prose explicitly denies a refreshed-Adam convergence guarantee; the reading recovers both the operational sequence and this adequate theorem/practice boundary.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P06 Algorithm 2 and Section 4.2 support leading left singular vectors, P^T G, compact moments, scaling, and lifting to dense weights. Section 4.1 supports changing-subspace accumulation and both refresh-frequency hazards. Theorem 3.8 has fixed projections and an identity regularizer. The reader accurately retains the prose's mechanism and its distinction between conditional projected-gradient contraction and unproved refreshed-Adam convergence.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

144 words; within budget

Adam's two moment matrices normally match the weight matrix in size, making gradient statistics a substantial memory cost. For a weight matrix with m <= n, GaLore takes the leading r left singular vectors of its gradient G as a projection P and forms R = P^T G. Adam stores and normalizes its moments in this r-by-n space instead of the original m-by-n space; a scaled update is then lifted through P and applied directly to the dense weights. Periodically recomputing P lets successive updates explore different subspaces without enlarging the moment arrays. Infrequent refreshes can miss useful directions, whereas frequent refreshes add decomposition work and can misalign accumulated statistics. For the specified gradient form, the theorem gives projected-gradient contraction with fixed projections, Lipschitz continuity, bounded weights, a contraction condition, and an identity gradient regularizer. This is not a convergence guarantee for periodically refreshed Adam.

Shared initial draft

For a weight matrix with m <= n, GaLore obtains a rank-r left projection P from the leading left singular vectors of its gradient G. It forms R = P^T G and stores Adam's first and second moments in this smaller space, then applies a scaled, expanded update to the dense weight matrix. Thus the compressed objects are optimizer statistics, not a permanently low-rank weight factorization. Recomputing P at scheduled intervals lets accumulated updates span different subspaces while keeping state dimensions fixed. This refresh is consequential: the fixed-projection theorem concerns contraction within the selected subspace and does not establish convergence of the periodically changing Adam procedure. Refreshing too rarely can leave useful gradient directions outside that subspace; refreshing too often adds decomposition cost and can disrupt the usefulness of accumulated moments. The design therefore trades projection rank and refresh frequency against memory and optimization progress.

P06 / Results / Sample 2

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: concern

162 words; within budget

The measured 22.0 GB footprint of 8-bit GaLore for 7B training makes small token batches feasible within a 24 GB memory budget, but it reflects more than gradient projection. Relative to BF16 Adam and 8-bit Adam, the reductions are 63.3% and 52.3%, respectively, in single-device profiling without checkpointing or offloading. Projection reduces optimizer-state storage, and per-layer updates avoid retaining all gradients. The close perplexity result comes from a separate experiment: a single trial on 64 A100 GPUs reaches 14.65 versus 14.61 for 8-bit Adam after 19.7 billion tokens. It supports close observed performance, not statistical equivalence or demonstrated complete pretraining on a consumer GPU. Throughput also constrains the memory benefit. At a 256-token batch for a 1B model, with both methods using per-layer updates, 8-bit GaLore processes 1019.63 tokens per second versus 1205.31 for 8-bit Adam. The benefit is therefore access to a lower-memory training regime, with a throughput penalty in the measured implementation.

P05 / Abstract / Sample 3

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

176 words; within budget

Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation (DoRA) separates changes in pretrained weight magnitude from low-rank directional updates, giving scale adjustment its own small parameter vector. The design is motivated by a VL-BART analysis in which LoRA's magnitude and direction changes are positively correlated, unlike the negatively correlated changes of full fine-tuning. DoRA normalizes the updated directions and learns their magnitudes separately; the resulting weights can be merged before inference. Across eight commonsense reasoning tasks, LLaMA-7B's average accuracy rises from 74.7% with LoRA to 78.4% with DoRA at the same rank, with trainable fractions of 0.83% and 0.84%. This comparison uses a prior-work LoRA baseline and an adjusted DoRA learning rate. Halving DoRA's rank still yields 77.5% accuracy with 0.43% of parameters trainable, supporting the value of separating magnitude learning under a smaller directional budget. Multimodal averages also improve over LoRA, although VL-BART's video-task average remains below full fine-tuning. Detaching the normalization denominator reduces training overhead: relative to exact normalization, a LLaMA-7B ablation lowers memory by 24.4% with a 0.2-percentage-point accuracy decrease.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: DoRA separates learned magnitudes from normalized low-rank directions, motivated by LoRA's differing magnitude-direction correlation from full fine-tuning. It improves accuracy at similar or smaller trainable budgets and allows inference merging.

Scope: Eight-task LLaMA-7B accuracy is 78.4% versus 74.7% at equal rank; the LoRA baseline is prior work and DoRA's learning rate differs. Half-rank gives 77.5% with 0.43% trainable parameters. Multimodal gains do not close the video full-fine-tuning gap. Denominator detachment saves 24.4% memory with a 0.2-percentage-point accuracy decrease.

Witness: giving scale adjustment its own small parameter vector

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

P05 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

DoRA: Weight-Decomposed Low-Rank Adaptation

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

123 words; within budget

DoRA gives column rescaling its own parameters instead of requiring a low-rank matrix update to represent both scale and direction changes. Each adapted column is a learned magnitude multiplied by the normalized sum of the frozen pretrained column and a low-rank update. The magnitude begins at the pretrained column norm, and the low-rank product begins at zero, so adaptation starts from the original model. Normalization assigns scale to the magnitude vector while the factors alter direction. To reduce gradient-graph storage, the implementation recomputes the normalization denominator but detaches it during backpropagation. This retains the forward parameterization and magnitude gradient, while changing the exact direction gradient from a scaled orthogonal projection to scaling alone. After training, the resulting weights can be merged for inference.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: The implementation recomputes but detaches denominators to save gradient-graph storage. Forward parameterization and magnitude gradients remain, but directional gradients become scaling alone instead of exact scaled orthogonal projections. Adapted weights can merge for inference.

135 words; within budget

DoRA adds a low-rank matrix product to the frozen pretrained weights, normalizes each resulting column, and multiplies it by a trainable magnitude. Normalization removes the column-length change introduced by the matrix update before the separate scalar sets its size. Thus, the low-rank factors supply directional adaptation rather than having to represent all scale adjustments as well. Magnitudes start at the pretrained column norms and the low-rank product starts at zero, preserving the original model at initialization. To reduce backpropagation memory, practical DoRA recomputes the normalization denominators but detaches them from the gradient graph. This preserves the normalized forward weights and magnitude gradient while replacing the exact directional gradient's projection with scaling. The evaluated implementation therefore retains the separation in its weight representation without using the full normalization derivative. The effective weights are merged for inference.

Reviewer 1: Tie A explains why normalization removes scale; B expresses the derivative change directly as 'scaling alone.' P05 Eqs. (5)-(7) and (11) support both accounts of initialization, forward weights, and unchanged magnitude learning. Both distinguish the practical derivative from exact normalization and retain inference merging. The explanatory emphases differ, but neither materially improves the methods function.

Reviewer 2: Tie 'Normalization assigns scale' in A and 'removes the column-length change' in B explain the same separation in P05 Eq. 5. Shared initial draft

DoRA represents each adapted weight column as a learned magnitude multiplied by a normalized direction. The direction is obtained by adding a low-rank matrix product to the frozen pretrained weights; the magnitude vector is initialized from their column norms, and the low-rank product starts at zero. Consequently, adaptation begins from the original model while assigning scale changes to a small separate parameter vector. This separation lets the low-rank factors alter direction without also having to encode every magnitude adjustment. The practical training procedure recomputes the normalization denominator but detaches it during backpropagation, avoiding the additional gradient graph required by exact normalization. This retains the forward reparameterization and magnitude gradient, but replaces the exact direction gradient's projection with a scaling operation. After training, the normalized and rescaled weights are merged for deployment.

P06 / Abstract / Sample 3

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

167 words; within budget

GaLore targets optimizer-state memory without imposing a low-rank parameterization on language-model weights. Its central distinction is that full-rank weights can be updated through compact gradient representations: optimizer statistics are maintained in a projected space, and processed updates are mapped back to the original matrix. Periodically refreshing that space lets accumulated updates span more than one subspace. The analysis provides conditional bounds on gradient stable rank and convergence of projected gradients under fixed projections without adaptive regularization. Experiments examine C4 pretraining and pretrained-model fine-tuning. In a single untuned 7B comparison on 64 A100 GPUs over 19.7 billion tokens, 8-bit GaLore reaches validation perplexity 14.65 versus 14.61 for 8-bit Adam. A separate device-memory measurement combines projection, 8-bit optimizer states, and per-layer updates, requiring 22.0 GB at a token batch size of 256 without offloading or activation checkpointing. This fits within 24 GB capacity. The results support lower-memory full-weight training with near-baseline perplexity in the reported 7B run, but lower throughput in the measured 1B implementation.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: GaLore optimizes compact projected gradients and lifts updates to full-rank weights. Refreshed subspaces expand accumulated changes, reducing optimizer-state memory without low-rank weight constraints.

Scope: One untuned 7B C4 run on 64 A100s over 19.7B tokens gives perplexity 14.65 versus Adam's 14.61. Separate 22.0 GB profiling combines projection, 8-bit states, per-layer updates, batch 256, and no offloading/checkpointing. Bounds concern conditional fixed-projection behavior; measured 1B throughput decreases.

P06 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

134 words; within budget

GaLore reduces Adam's storage by accumulating moments of a projected gradient rather than of every weight coordinate. For an m-by-n gradient with m <= n, retaining the leading singular vectors yields an m-by-r projector; multiplying by its transpose produces an r-by-n representation. Adam maintains its first and second moments in that smaller space, then the processed update is scaled and mapped back into the full weight matrix. The opposite projection side is used when m > n. Periodic recomputation lets later updates follow different subspaces, so accumulated weight changes are not confined to one rank-r factorization. The formal convergence result concerns fixed projections without adaptive regularization: projected gradients contract when retained-subspace curvature exceeds the continuity-dependent threshold and the learning rate permits contraction. It establishes progress within that subspace, not convergence of the periodically refreshed Adam procedure.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: GaLore projects gradients onto leading singular vectors, stores Adam moments in the smaller representation, and lifts processed updates to dense weights. Refreshing projections lets accumulated changes escape a single low-rank factorization.

Scope: For m <= n, an m-by-r projector produces r-by-n gradients; the opposite side is used otherwise. Contraction requires fixed projections, no adaptive regularization, sufficient retained-subspace curvature, and a suitable learning rate. It establishes subspace progress, not convergence of refreshed Adam.

Witness: not convergence of the periodically refreshed Adam procedure

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P06 Sec. 4.2/Algorithm 2 support the projector dimensions, compact moments and dimension-dependent projection side; Sec. 4.1 supports refreshed update directions. Theorem 3.8/Eq. 11 support conditional contraction under fixed projections, identity regularization and a curvature/continuity threshold. Prose additionally requires a contraction-permitting learning rate and denies a refreshed-Adam guarantee. The reading recovers these selected operational and theoretical boundaries; omitted detailed assumptions are not reconstructed or credited.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P06 Section 4.2 and Algorithm 2 support the projector dimensions, smaller moments, lifting, scaling, and opposite projection side. Section 4.1 supports refresh beyond one subspace. Theorem 3.8's contraction bound supports the fixed-projection, identity-regularizer, curvature, and suitable-step-size qualifications. The reader recovers the operation and accurately restricts the guarantee to designated-subspace progress rather than periodically refreshed Adam.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

135 words; within budget

GaLore uses singular value decomposition to select a compact subspace from the current gradient. For a rectangular weight matrix, its implementation projects the gradient on one side, chosen from the matrix dimensions. Adam's first and second moments then occupy this reduced space rather than matching the full weight matrix. The optimizer processes the projected gradient, maps the update back, and adds it directly to the weights. Periodic recomputation of the projection admits new update directions while keeping the moment storage compact; no separate trainable adapter is needed. The fixed-projection analysis addresses a narrower operation: without adaptive regularization, the projected gradient contracts under the stated continuity and bounded-weight assumptions when retained curvature exceeds a continuity-dependent threshold and the step size yields contraction. This describes progress inside a designated subspace; the switching Adam implementation is evaluated empirically.

Shared initial draft

For a weight matrix, GaLore takes a singular value decomposition of the current gradient and retains a rank-limited projection. The implementation projects on one side of the gradient, choosing that side from the matrix dimensions, so Adam's first and second moments occupy the reduced space. The processed update is then mapped back and added directly to the full weight matrix. P06 / Results / Sample 3

GaLore: Memory-Efficient LLM Training by Gradient Low-Rank Projection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

170 words; within budget

At 1B parameters, GaLore preserves much of the full-rank baseline's C4 performance while reducing optimizer storage, rather than shrinking the weight matrix. With rank 512 and 13.1 billion training tokens, validation perplexity is 15.64, compared with 15.56 for full-rank Adam and 19.21 for same-rank LoRA. Estimated weight-and-optimizer storage falls to 4.38 GB, versus 7.80 GB and 6.17 GB, respectively. The quality comparison therefore favors compressing gradient statistics over restricting learned changes to a single low-rank factorization in this setting. The separate 7B device footprint of 22.0 GB also relies on 8-bit states and immediate per-layer updates at a small token batch size; it is not a projection-only saving. A corresponding 1B measurement at 256 tokens makes the runtime tradeoff explicit: memory decreases from 6.93 GB with 8-bit Adam to 5.63 GB with 8-bit GaLore, while throughput falls from 1205.31 to 1019.63 tokens per second. Lower storage thus enables a different memory budget, not faster training in this measurement.

P07 / Abstract / Sample 1

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

172 words; within budget

A larger context window does not establish that a language model can use evidence wherever it appears. We test this distinction through multi-document question answering and synthetic key-value retrieval, changing context length and evidence position while preserving the required answer. Across the evaluated models, question-answering accuracy generally peaks at the beginning or end of the context and falls when the answer-bearing passage moves to the middle; in some GPT-3.5-Turbo settings, supplied evidence yields lower accuracy than answering without documents. Extended-context versions show little advantage when the input fits both versions' windows. Several models also struggle with middle-position UUID pairs, although both evaluated Claude variants retrieve them nearly perfectly. Repeating the query before and after the data largely eliminates synthetic retrieval errors but leaves the question-answering pattern substantially unchanged. In an open-domain question-answering case study, reader accuracy saturates while retrieval recall continues to improve. Together, these comparisons distinguish input capacity from effective evidence use: neither a larger window, additional retrieved documents, nor improved exact matching necessarily translates into better contextual question answering.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Position-controlled question answering and UUID retrieval distinguish accepting context from using evidence: middle-position evidence often lowers accuracy, longer windows offer little advantage on shared-length inputs, and query repetition fixes lookup without fixing question answering. Better retrieval recall also need not improve reader accuracy.

Scope: Evaluated models and tasks only; some GPT-3.5-Turbo document conditions underperform closed-book answers; both evaluated Claude variants retrieve UUIDs nearly perfectly.

Witness: distinguish input capacity from effective evidence use

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P07 sections 2.3, 3.2, 4.2, and 5 support all four contrasts: position, shared-window capacity, lookup intervention, and recall versus reader gains. Prose uses 'generally,' 'several,' and 'necessarily' and names the Claude exception. Reading recovers the evidence-use relation and explicit evaluated-model, shared-length, and task distinctions. These qualifiers adequately bound the selected abstract conclusion without requiring all numerical configurations.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P07 Secs. 2.1/3.1 support answer-preserving position/length tests. Sec. 4.2 supports repetition repairing lookup but not QA; Sec. 5 supports saturation despite improving recall. Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

170 words; within budget

Expanding a language model's context window does not necessarily improve its use of information within that window. We examine this distinction through multi-document question answering and synthetic key-value retrieval, changing the position of relevant information and the amount of distracting content while preserving the required answer. Question-answering accuracy generally peaks when the answer-bearing passage comes first or last and declines when it appears in the middle; in the worst tested placements, GPT-3.5-Turbo performs below its closed-book accuracy. Extended-context counterparts show little advantage when the inputs fit both models' windows. Several models exhibit similar positional weaknesses on random UUID retrieval, although both Claude variants perform nearly perfectly. Repeating the query before and after the data largely resolves synthetic retrieval errors but leaves question-answering positional sensitivity substantially unchanged. In a complementary open-domain evaluation, reader accuracy saturates before retrieval recall, limiting the benefit of additional documents. Together, these controlled contrasts show that accommodating more tokens and retrieving a matching value are insufficient indicators of effective evidence use in the evaluated question-answering settings.

A larger context window does not establish that a language model can use evidence wherever it appears. We investigate this distinction through controlled tests of multi-document question answering and synthetic key-value retrieval, varying both context length and the location of the information needed for a fixed answer. P07 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

144 words; within budget

To test whether models use evidence independently of its location, we relocate the answer-bearing paragraph while holding the question, document set, and required answer fixed. For 2,655 NaturalQuestions-Open queries with paragraph annotations, contexts contain that paragraph and retrieved Wikipedia distractors excluding annotated answers. Reordering tests position at a fixed length; varying the total from 10 to 20 or 30 documents tests the effect of additional distractors. Closed-book prompts measure accuracy without supplied evidence, and oracle prompts with only the answer paragraph provide a reference without distractor competition. Synthetic retrieval replaces documents with serialized JSON containing random UUID keys and values, reducing linguistic confounders while retaining the need to locate one relevant item. We vary its position across 75, 140, or 300 pairs, using 500 examples per setting. With greedy decoding, both tasks score whether a correct answer or value appears in the output.

Witness: Reordering tests position at a fixed length

'Excluding annotated answers' preserves the source's filtering criterion without falsely guaranteeing absence of every reasonable alternative answer.

P07 / Results / Sample 1

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

163 words; within budget

Query placement distinguishes successful token matching from successful use of evidence. With 300 UUID key-value pairs, placing the query before and after the data gives GPT-3.5-Turbo (16K) perfect retrieval accuracy, compared with a worst-case accuracy of 45.6% without that intervention. In multi-document question answering, however, the same prompt change slightly improves accuracy when evidence comes first and slightly decreases it elsewhere, leaving the positional pattern substantially intact. Making the query available during context processing therefore helps this synthetic lookup task without resolving the observed difficulty with natural-language evidence. Training length further changes the interpretation: Flan-UL2 has only a 1.9-percentage-point spread across evidence positions within its 2,048-token training window, but develops a middle-position disadvantage on longer inputs. GPT-3.5-Turbo and its 16K counterpart also have nearly overlapping accuracy curves on the 10- and 20-document inputs that fit both windows. These comparisons make task demands and training context relevant to evaluating long-context capability, rather than treating nominal window size as sufficient.

Scope: 300 UUID pairs; Flan-UL2's within-window positional spread is 1.9 percentage points at 2,048 tokens. Standard/16K comparison uses 10/20 documents fitting both windows.

Shared initial draft

With 300 key-value pairs, GPT-3.5-Turbo (16K) reaches perfect retrieval accuracy, whereas its worst accuracy without this change is 45.6%. P08 / Abstract / Sample 1

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

179 words; within budget

Retrieved passages can improve factual answers without ensuring that generated claims are supported by the passages cited. Self-RAG addresses this mismatch by training a language model to encode retrieval decisions, passage relevance, evidential support, and response utility in reflection tokens generated alongside response text. A separately trained critic supplies annotations offline; at inference, the generator evaluates its own passage-conditioned continuations and ranks them using generation probabilities and critique scores. This makes predicted evidence support part of sentence selection, with adjustable priorities rather than a fixed retrieve-and-generate pipeline. Zero-shot evaluations of trained 7B and 13B models cover factual question answering, classification, biographies, and cited long-form answers. Against a 7B model trained on the same instruction-output pairs but augmented with retrieval only at test time, Self-RAG improves long-tail PopQA accuracy from 48.7% to 54.9%. On ASQA, its citation precision exceeds retrieval-augmented ChatGPT, while citation recall and answer correctness remain lower. Increasing the support weight improves precision but reduces measured fluency. The results support controllable evidence use while showing that better attribution need not improve every dimension of answer quality.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Self-RAG generates retrieval and critique tokens, then uses its own support-aware scores to select passage-conditioned sentences. Offline critic supervision enables adjustable evidence use; gains in attribution or factual accuracy do not imply uniformly better answers.

Scope: Zero-shot 7B/13B evaluations span factual QA, classification, biographies, and cited long-form answers. Same-data 7B PopQA accuracy rises 48.7% to 54.9%; ASQA precision beats ChatGPT but recall/correctness lag, and stronger support weighting reduces fluency.

Witness: makes predicted evidence support part of sentence selection

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P08 sections 3.1-3.3 support offline critic supervision and generator-scored selection; Table 2 and section 5.2 support the PopQA and ASQA tradeoffs. Prose calls support 'predicted' and rejects improvement in every answer dimension. Reading recovers this mechanism and the zero-shot 7B/13B, same-data 7B, and metric-specific comparison boundaries. Both ASQA model rows support the unnumbered precision/recall/correctness contrast; no complete-grounding guarantee is asserted.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P08 Secs. 3.1-3.3 support offline critic supervision, generator-owned reflection, and probability-plus-critique sentence selection. Secs. 4.1/4.3 and Table 2 support zero-shot 7B/13B evaluation and the same-data PopQA 48.7%-to-54.9% comparison. Table 2 supports greater ASQA precision but lower recall/correctness than Ret-ChatGPT; Sec. 5.2 supports the support-weight/fluency tradeoff. Reading preserves adjustable predicted support and nonuniform quality gains.

P08 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

135 words; within budget

Self-RAG selects among sentences conditioned on different retrieved passages, using predicted evidence support as a decoding criterion. A Llama 2-7B critic trained on GPT-4 feedback annotates instruction-output pairs with retrieval decisions and judgments of relevance, support, and utility. The generator learns response text and these reflection tokens together through next-token prediction, with retrieved passages excluded from the training loss. At inference, a normalized retrieval-token probability is compared with a threshold. If retrieval is triggered, the generator produces separate candidate sentences from different passages, then combines each candidate's generation probability with weighted probabilities of favorable critique tokens. Segment-level beam search retains the highest-scoring continuations. This lets predicted support influence which claims enter the answer and allows the relevance, support, and utility priorities to change without retraining. The critic provides offline supervision rather than additional inference-time evaluations.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Train the generator to emit text and offline critic judgments; trigger retrieval by thresholding normalized retrieval-token probability. Generate sentence candidates per passage and retain high-scoring continuations using segment-level beam search, so predicted support affects claims selected.

Scope: Llama 2-7B critic trained on GPT-4 feedback; next-token training excludes passage text from loss. Candidate scores combine generation and weighted favorable-critique probabilities; priorities change without retraining or inference-time critic evaluation.

Witness: using predicted evidence support as a decoding criterion

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose's 'predicted evidence support' makes the epistemic boundary explicit. Reading recovers the offline-to-generator mechanism and adjustable priorities without inference-time critic evaluation. This method-focused scope is adequate; no specific beam width, evaluation result, or guaranteed grounding is claimed.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

135 words; within budget

Self-RAG uses offline critique to make evidence judgments available during generation. A Llama 2-7B critic trained on GPT-4 feedback annotates instruction-output pairs with retrieval decisions and relevance, support, and utility tokens. The generator learns response text and these tokens through next-token prediction, while retrieved passages are masked from the loss. At inference, a threshold on the retrieval-token probability determines whether to request passages. If retrieval occurs, each passage conditions a separate candidate sentence; segment-level beam search ranks these continuations using generation scores plus a weighted sum of normalized critique-token probabilities. The predicted support score can therefore influence which claim is selected, rather than support being presumed from the availability of a relevant passage. Adjusting the support weight or retrieval threshold changes inference behavior without further training, and the separate critic is not needed for generation.

Shared initial draft

A critic initialized from Llama 2-7B is trained on GPT-4 feedback, then annotates instruction-output pairs with retrieval decisions and judgments of relevance, support, and utility. Standard next-token training teaches the generator to produce both response text and reflection tokens; retrieved passage tokens are excluded from the loss. At inference, a retrieval-token probability threshold determines whether external evidence is requested. For each sentence, the model can generate separate continuations conditioned on different retrieved passages, then use segment-level beam search to favor candidates with higher generation and critique scores. This operation evaluates support at the point where competing claims are selected, rather than treating retrieval alone as validation. Critique weights can be changed at test time, and the training critic is not needed during generation.

P08 / Results / Sample 1

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

173 words; within budget

A baseline trained on the same instruction-output pairs does not reproduce Self-RAG's attribution gains. Llama2-FT7B is trained on those pairs without passages or reflection tokens and receives retrieval only at test time. Self-RAG 7B raises long-tail PopQA accuracy from 48.7% to 54.9% and ASQA citation precision from 5.0% to 66.9%, yet ASQA correctness (str-em) falls from 31.0% to 30.0%. The combination therefore improves attribution without uniformly improving answer content. On the same five ASQA input documents, Self-RAG 13B exceeds retrieval-augmented ChatGPT in citation precision, 70.3% versus 65.1%, but trails in recall, 71.3% versus 76.6%, and answer correctness. Its stronger precision does not establish broader evidence coverage. The inference controls expose the same need to choose an objective: raising the support weight improves citation precision while lowering MAUVE, the reported fluency measure. Reducing retrieval frequency also harms PopQA accuracy more than PubHealth accuracy. Thus, the value of selective retrieval and critique depends on both the task and the aspect of output quality being prioritized.
