P07 / Abstract / Sample 2

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

183 words; within budget

Accepting a long input does not establish that a language model can use evidence throughout it. We diagnose this distinction with multi-document question answering and synthetic key-value retrieval, varying context length and the position of the relevant item while preserving the required answer. In the evaluated question-answering models, placing the answer passage in the middle generally lowers accuracy relative to placing it at either end; some settings fall below answering without documents, even though the answer passage is present. Extended-context variants perform almost identically to their shorter-context counterparts when both can accommodate the same input. Repeating the query before and after the context largely eliminates synthetic lookup failures but leaves the question-answering position pattern intact, showing that successful token retrieval need not entail effective evidence use. Encoder-decoder models are less position-sensitive within their training-time context lengths, yet also develop middle-context degradation on longer inputs. In a standard retrieval pipeline, reader accuracy saturates while retrieval recall continues to rise. These converging tests identify evidence access and use, rather than accepted context length or answer availability alone, as a bottleneck in the evaluated question-answering settings.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Position/context-length diagnostics show that accepting longer inputs does not ensure evidence use: middle passages hurt QA, larger windows offer little on shared inputs, and improved lookup leaves QA positional failures. Saturating reader accuracy despite rising recall identifies an evidence-use bottleneck.

Scope: Multi-document QA and synthetic key-value tasks preserve the answer. Encoder-decoder robustness holds within training context lengths but degrades beyond them; some QA cases underperform no-document answering.

Witness: successful token retrieval need not entail effective evidence use

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose confines its bottleneck claim to evaluated QA settings and uses 'some' for below-closed-book results. Reading recovers the central access/use distinction and within-versus-beyond-training boundary, with shared-input comparisons in its core account. The selected synthesis need not reproduce every anchor exception to avoid universal claims.

Secs. 4.1/4.2 support training-length-dependent encoder-decoder robustness and query repetition's lookup/QA contrast. Sec. 5 supports reader saturation while recall rises. Reading retains the prose's evaluated-QA limitation and intervention/training boundaries, correctly locating the bottleneck in evidence use rather than nominal capacity.

P07 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

151 words; within budget

Moving the answer passage while holding the question and available documents fixed tests whether evidence availability is enough for successful answering. The study uses 2,655 NaturalQuestions-Open queries whose annotated long answers are paragraphs. Each prompt contains one annotated answer passage and relevance-ranked distractors filtered to exclude annotated answers. Placing the answer passage at different positions in 10-, 20-, and 30-document contexts tests position sensitivity; changing the distractor count tests the effect of added context without changing the target answer. Closed-book prompts establish performance without supplied evidence, while an oracle prompt supplies only the answer passage. A complementary task uses unique random UUID key-value pairs to reduce linguistic confounds and test exact lookup. Both tasks use greedy decoding and score inclusion of a correct answer or value. Repeating the query before and after the data probes whether allowing earlier tokens to be contextualized with the query changes these two tasks differently.

P07 / Results / Sample 2

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

155 words; within budget

Query repetition distinguishes an exact-lookup failure from a broader difficulty using retrieved text. With 300 UUID key-value pairs, GPT-3.5-Turbo (16K) achieves 100% accuracy when the query appears on both sides of the data, whereas its worst-position accuracy with the query only afterward is 45.6%. In multi-document question answering, however, repetition leaves the positional pattern largely unchanged: it slightly helps when evidence comes first and slightly hurts other placements. Query-aware prompting therefore does not provide a general remedy for the tested evidence-use problem. Some GPT-3.5-Turbo settings with 20 or 30 documents fall below its 56.1% closed-book accuracy despite containing an answer passage, so supplying evidence among distractors can be worse than supplying none. In the separate retrieval-pipeline study, adding documents beyond 20 produces only marginal reader gains while retrieval recall continues to improve. Together, these comparisons show that successful lookup and higher retrieval recall do not, by themselves, establish effective question answering.

P08 / Abstract / Sample 2

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

177 words; within budget

Retrieved text can be relevant to a query without supporting the claims in an answer. SELF-RAG makes that distinction part of generation: a language model learns when to retrieve and uses predicted relevance, support, and utility to select sentence continuations. A critic trained on GPT-4 feedback annotates instruction-output pairs offline; the generator learns text and reflection tokens together with a standard next-token objective. At inference, it supplies its own assessments, allowing retrieval frequency and evidential emphasis to be adjusted without retraining or calling the critic. Zero-shot evaluation of 7B and 13B Llama 2 models covers six tasks spanning factual questions, closed-set decisions, biographies, and cited long-form answers. With the same five supplied ASQA documents, SELF-RAG 7B achieves 66.9% citation precision, compared with 5.0% for Llama2-FT 7B, trained on the same instruction-output pairs but using retrieval only at inference. However, its str-em correctness is lower, and increasing the support weight raises citation precision while lowering MAUVE, the reported fluency metric. The evidence supports improved attribution and adjustable tradeoffs, rather than a uniform gain across response qualities.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: SELF-RAG learns retrieval decisions and separate relevance/support/utility judgments to select sentences, letting users adjust evidence emphasis without retraining or an inference-time critic. This improves attribution while exposing correctness and fluency tradeoffs.

Scope: Offline GPT-4-feedback critic; zero-shot 7B/13B Llama 2 evaluation on six tasks. With five identical ASQA documents and matched instruction-output data, 7B precision is 66.9% versus 5.0%, but correctness is lower; increased support weight lowers MAUVE.

Witness: rather than a uniform gain across response qualities

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P08 Table 1 and sections 3.2-3.3 support distinct relevance/support judgments, offline GPT-4-derived supervision, and adjustable decoding without the critic. Table 2 and section 5.2 confirm 66.9 versus 5.0 precision, lower correctness, and the MAUVE cost. Reading recovers these relations and the explicit zero-shot six-task, 7B/13B, matched-data/five-document boundaries. 'Rather than a uniform gain' adequately limits the selected attribution claim.

Sec. 5.2 supports lower MAUVE under stronger support weighting. The reading recovers the relevance/support distinction and bounded attribution advantage.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

182 words; within budget

Retrieval can supply relevant text without ensuring that a generated claim is supported by it. SELF-RAG addresses this mismatch by training a language model to request retrieval and assess passage relevance, evidential support, and response utility through reflection tokens. A critic trained on GPT-4 feedback annotates instruction-output pairs offline; the generator learns both text and evaluations with a standard next-token objective. At inference, it generates sentence continuations for retrieved passages and ranks them using generation probabilities and weighted self-evaluations. Selection can therefore favor continuations judged better supported rather than rely on passage relevance alone, with retrieval frequency and support emphasis adjustable without retraining. Llama 2 models with 7B and 13B parameters are evaluated zero-shot on six tasks. On ASQA, the 7B model achieves 66.9% citation precision versus 5.0% for Llama2-FT 7B, which uses the same instruction-output training pairs but adds retrieval only at inference; both receive the same five documents. However, str-em correctness is slightly lower, at 30.0% versus 31.0%. Increasing the support weight also improves citation precision while reducing MAUVE, exposing a tradeoff rather than uniform improvement.

Scope: Zero-shot Llama 2 7B/13B on six tasks; same-data ASQA 7B comparison uses identical five documents: precision 66.9% versus 5.0%, str-em 30.0% versus 31.0%. Higher support weighting reduces MAUVE.

Shared initial draft

Retrieval can supply relevant text without ensuring that a generated claim is supported by it. SELF-RAG addresses this distinction by teaching a language model to decide when to retrieve and to evaluate passage relevance, evidential support, and response utility through dedicated reflection tokens. A critic trained on GPT-4 feedback annotates instruction-output data offline; the generator then learns text and reflection tokens with a standard next-token objective. During inference, sentence-level candidate continuations are ranked using generation probabilities and weighted self-evaluations, allowing retrieval frequency and evidential emphasis to be adjusted without retraining. Llama 2 models with 7B and 13B parameters are evaluated zero-shot on six tasks spanning factual questions, closed-set decisions, biographies, and cited long-form answers. On ASQA, the 7B model obtains 66.9% citation precision versus 5.0% for a model trained on the same instruction-output pairs with retrieval added only at inference, using the same five supplied documents. These gains do not imply improvement on every measure: ASQA str-em correctness remains below that baseline, and increasing the support weight improves citation precision while reducing MAUVE, the fluency metric.

P08 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

149 words; within budget

SELF-RAG learns evidence judgments alongside response text so that a separate critic is unnecessary at inference. During data preparation, a Llama 2 critic trained on GPT-4 reflection labels decides whether a target segment needs retrieval, labels passage relevance, and assesses support for that segment; response utility is judged independently of the passages. The generator learns from these augmented examples using next-token prediction, with retrieved passage tokens excluded from the loss. At inference, it produces a candidate sentence and critique tokens for each retrieved passage. Segment-level beam search ranks continuations using generation probability plus a weighted sum of normalized probabilities for favorable relevance, support, and utility labels, rather than simply accepting the top retrieved passage. The support weight can therefore change which continuation is selected, while a threshold on the normalized retrieval-token probability controls whether evidence is requested. These operations make learned assessments adjustable selection criteria without another training run.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Offline critic labels teach the generator evidence judgments alongside text. At inference, segment-level beam search combines generation probability with normalized favorable relevance, support, and utility probabilities to rank passage-conditioned sentences; a normalized retrieval-probability threshold controls evidence requests. Learned assessments become adjustable selection criteria without retraining or an inference-time critic.

Scope: Llama 2 critic trained on GPT-4 reflection labels; passage tokens are excluded from loss; utility is passage-independent; support weights can change selected continuations.

Witness: rather than simply accepting the top retrieved passage

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P08 Table 1, sections 3.2-3.3, and equations 2-4 support passage-independent utility, loss masking, normalized retrieval thresholding, and weighted favorable-token beam scores. Prose contrasts candidate ranking with simply accepting the top passage. Reading recovers that mechanism and the offline critic/no-retraining boundaries. The paragraph describes learned assessments as selection criteria, not certified grounding; evaluation settings are unnecessary to this selected method claim.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. Sec. 3.3, Eqs. 3-4, supports favorable critique probabilities normalized by category, weighted segment-level beam ranking, and a normalized retrieval-token threshold. Reading recovers the prose's adjustable assessment-to-selection mechanism and does not treat model-predicted support as guaranteed factual support.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

140 words; within budget

SELF-RAG trains the generator to supply the judgments later used to select its own continuations, avoiding a separate critic call at inference. Offline, a Llama 2 critic distilled from GPT-4 feedback marks retrieval needs, passage relevance, support for each target segment, and overall response utility. The generator learns ordinary text and these reflection tokens together; retrieved passages remain conditioning context but are excluded from the training loss. At inference, each retrieved passage conditions a candidate sentence. Segment-level beam search scores candidates using generation probabilities plus weighted, normalized probabilities of favorable relevance, support, and utility tokens. A relevant passage can accompany a weakly supported continuation; scoring support separately lets decoding prefer continuations it judges better grounded. Changing these weights or the retrieval threshold alters decoding behavior without retraining, separating whether to obtain evidence from how strongly support should influence answer selection.

Shared initial draft

SELF-RAG turns evidence checking into a learned part of generation rather than a separate test-time critic call. A Llama 2 critic, trained on GPT-4 reflection labels, first decides whether each training segment needs retrieval. Retrieved passages receive relevance labels, and relevant passages are paired with judgments of support for the target segment; a utility label assesses the response independently of those passages. The generator learns to predict ordinary text and reflection tokens from these augmented examples, while retrieved passage tokens are excluded from the loss. At inference, the generator produces its own evaluations. For each retrieved passage, it generates a candidate sentence and scores the continuation using its generation probability plus weighted, normalized probabilities of favorable critique tokens. Segment-level beam search retains promising continuations. This makes evidential support part of output selection while allowing its importance, or retrieval frequency, to be adjusted without another training run.

P08 / Results / Sample 2

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

178 words; within budget

SELF-RAG improves ASQA attribution without uniformly improving answer correctness. Using the same five supplied passages, its 7B model achieves citation precision and recall of 66.9% and 67.8%, versus 5.0% and 7.5% for Llama2-FT 7B, trained on the same instruction-output pairs with retrieval added only at inference. Yet str-em correctness is 30.0 versus 31.0. The contrast concerns how available evidence is incorporated, not access to more test-time documents. In a separate ablation with 50,000 training examples and 150 sampled ASQA questions, training on the top retrieved passage without reflection tokens yields str-em of 18.1, compared with 32.1 for SELF-RAG. The ablation changes passage handling as well as reflection supervision, so it supports the combined configuration rather than isolating critique tokens. Test-time controls expose further tradeoffs: increasing the support weight improves citation precision but reduces MAUVE, while raising the retrieval threshold reduces retrieval frequency and causes greater accuracy deterioration on PopQA than PubHealth. Thus, stronger attribution and less retrieval are choices with distinct costs, not interchangeable improvements in overall answer quality.

P07 / Abstract / Sample 3

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

183 words; within budget

A long context window specifies how much text a model can accept, not how consistently it can use the evidence supplied. This study tests that distinction by moving answer-bearing information through otherwise unchanged contexts and varying the number of distractors. Multi-document question answering on 2,655 NaturalQuestions-Open queries is paired with retrieval of random UUID key-value pairs to distinguish evidence use from exact matching. In question answering, the tested models generally favor evidence at the beginning or end over evidence in the middle; GPT-3.5-Turbo can perform worse with the correct passage among distractors than without any documents. Standard and extended-context counterparts have nearly identical positional profiles when inputs fit both windows. Yet retrieval failures are not uniform: Claude-1.3 variants perform nearly perfectly on the synthetic task. Placing the query both before and after the data brings all tested models close to perfect key-value retrieval but leaves the question-answering pattern largely unchanged. These contrasting responses show why neither a larger advertised window nor success at exact lookup establishes that a model will consistently locate and use answer evidence in the evaluated contexts.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Moving evidence and varying distractors reveals that context capacity and exact matching do not establish reliable evidence use: QA favors context ends, and query repetition repairs UUID lookup without repairing QA positional sensitivity.

Scope: 2,655 NaturalQuestions-Open queries plus random UUID retrieval. Shared-window comparisons show similar standard/extended profiles; Claude-1.3 nearly perfectly retrieves UUIDs. GPT-3.5-Turbo can underperform no-document QA despite supplied evidence.

Witness: how much text a model can accept

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P07 sections 2.1/2.3, 3.1-3.2, and 4.2 support position-controlled QA, UUID lookup, Claude exceptions, and differing intervention effects. Prose restricts comparisons to inputs fitting both windows and conclusions to 'evaluated contexts.' Reading recovers the capacity/use distinction, 2,655-query setting, Claude exception, and shared-window qualification. The selected abstract diagnosis does not require importing every experimental configuration from the anchor.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P07 Secs. 2.1/3.1 support answer-preserving position and distractor changes, 2,655 paragraph-answer queries, and UUID lookup. Secs. 2.3/3.2 support edge-favored QA, some below-closed-book GPT performance, shared-window similarity, and the Claude lookup exception. Sec. 4.2 supports query repetition nearly solving lookup without repairing QA. The reading retains the prose's capacity/use distinction and task/model exceptions; the abstract need not enumerate every setup detail.

P07 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

The evaluation changes evidence location without changing the question, the available answer, or the distractor set within each context-length condition. A complementary lookup task uses serialized JSON objects with 75, 140, or 300 random UUID key-value pairs and 500 examples per size. Moving the queried pair isolates position in a setting with minimal linguistic content. Both tasks use greedy decoding and score whether an annotated answer or target value appears in the output, rather than requiring an exact match to the entire response.

P07 / Results / Sample 3

Lost in the Middle: How Language Models Use Long Contexts

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

150 words; within budget

The question-answering controls show that evidence availability and successful evidence use are different outcomes. GPT-3.5-Turbo reaches 88.3% accuracy with only the answer-bearing document, yet its worst 20- and 30-document configurations fall below the 56.1% closed-book reference. Evidence availability alone is therefore insufficient to improve answer accuracy. The standard and 16K variants also have nearly overlapping positional curves for 10- and 20-document inputs that fit both windows, so extra capacity does not improve evidence use in these conditions. Query placement distinguishes a narrower retrieval difficulty from the question-answering failure. With 300 key-value pairs, placing the query before and after the data raises GPT-3.5-Turbo (16K) from 45.6% worst-case accuracy to perfect retrieval. The same intervention offers little improvement in question answering and slightly lowers accuracy outside the first document position. Making exact lookup easier therefore does not remove the positional difficulty of answering questions from supplied evidence.

P08 / Abstract / Sample 3

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

188 words; within budget

Retrieval can supply useful passages without ensuring that a response needs external evidence or that its claims are supported. Self-RAG trains a language model to express retrieval decisions and evidence judgments as reflection tokens alongside response text. A critic distilled from GPT-4 feedback supplies offline judgments of retrieval need, relevance, support, and utility; the generator then learns these judgments itself. At inference, sentence continuations generated from different passages compete under scores combining generation probability with the model's reflection probabilities. Evidence assessment therefore affects output selection without a separate inference-time critic, while retrieval thresholds and critique weights allow behavior to change without retraining. Zero-shot evaluations of 7B and 13B models span six tasks. On PopQA, Self-RAG improves over a baseline trained on the same instruction-output pairs with retrieval added only at inference. On ASQA, the 13B model exceeds retrieval-augmented ChatGPT in citation precision, 70.3 versus 65.1, but trails in citation recall, 71.3 versus 76.6. On the 7B model, increasing the support weight improves precision while lowering MAUVE fluency scores. These findings support controllable evidence-aware generation, but better attribution does not imply better coverage or fluency.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Self-RAG learns retrieval decisions and evidence judgments as reflection tokens, then uses their probabilities to rank passage-conditioned sentences without a separate inference-time critic. This enables adjustable evidence-aware generation, but improved attribution does not ensure coverage or fluency.

Scope: Zero-shot 7B/13B evaluation on six tasks; PopQA improves over a same-data baseline. ASQA 13B precision is 70.3 versus 65.1, recall 71.3 versus 76.6; 7B support weighting trades precision for MAUVE.

Witness: allow behavior to change without retraining

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

The final attribution-versus-coverage/fluency conclusion is narrower than uniform superiority or guaranteed support.

Sec. 5.2 explicitly analyzes support weighting on 7B and its MAUVE cost. Reading preserves the mechanism, model-specific comparisons, and attribution-versus-coverage/fluency boundary.

P08 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

144 words; within budget

Self-RAG uses reflection tokens as a learned interface between evidence assessment and sentence selection. A Llama 2 critic distilled from GPT-4 annotations labels instruction-output pairs offline, inserting retrieval decisions and, when passages are used, judgments of their relevance and support for the response. Utility tokens assess the response independently of the passage. The generator learns both text and reflection tokens through next-token prediction, with retrieved passage text masked from the loss. During inference, each retrieved passage yields a candidate sentence continuation. Segment-level beam search adds weighted reflection scores to the generation probability: each critique score is normalized within its token category, allowing relevance, support, and utility to influence which continuation survives. Thus, a passage is not merely extra prompt content; its judged relationship to the response affects selection. Changing the retrieval threshold or critique weights adjusts this process without retraining or calling the critic.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Offline critic labels train the generator to predict reflection tokens; during inference, segment-level beam search combines generation probabilities with normalized relevance, support, and utility scores to select passage-conditioned sentence continuations. Evidence judgments thereby affect selection, with thresholds and weights adjustable without retraining or an inference-time critic.

Scope: Llama 2 critic distilled from GPT-4 annotations; retrieved passage text is loss-masked; utility is judged independently of passages.

Witness: its judged relationship to the response affects selection

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P08 sections 3.1-3.3, Table 1, and equations 2-4 support offline annotation, passage-loss masking, passage-independent utility, and normalized weighted candidate scores. Prose's 'judged relationship ... affects selection' is recovered by the reader's beam-search explanation. Reading also preserves adjustable controls and no inference-time critic. These are adequate boundaries for the selected training-to-decoding method; it makes no task-wide accuracy or grounding guarantee.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P08 Sec. 3.2 supports the GPT-4-distilled Llama 2 critic, offline labels, joint next-token training, and passage-loss masking; Sec. 3.1's utility footnote makes utility passage-independent. Sec. 3.3, Eqs. 3-4, supports sentence-segment beam ranking with generation probability and within-category normalized critique scores. The reading recovers this assessment-to-selection mechanism and the prose's adjustable thresholds/weights without retraining or inference-time critic calls.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

138 words; within budget

Relevance alone cannot establish whether a passage supports a generated claim, so Self-RAG evaluates relevance and support separately. A Llama 2 critic trained on GPT-4 judgments annotates instruction-output examples offline with retrieval and critique tokens. The generator learns to produce both response text and these judgments through next-token prediction, excluding retrieved passage text from the loss. When retrieval is triggered at inference, each passage conditions a candidate continuation for the next sentence. Segment-level beam search ranks candidates using generation probability plus weighted, normalized probabilities for relevance, support, and utility. A continuation based on a relevant passage can therefore receive a lower score if its claims are only partially supported. Evidence assessment changes which continuation is selected, rather than supplying feedback after selection. Adjusting the retrieval threshold or critique weights changes generation behavior without retraining or invoking the critic.

Shared initial draft

Self-RAG turns evidence assessment into tokens that the generator can predict, rather than an external judgment requested after each response. A Llama 2 critic learns reflection categories from GPT-4 annotations and labels instruction-output pairs offline. When a segment needs retrieval, passages and judgments of relevance and support are inserted into its training sequence; utility judgments evaluate the response. The generator is trained by next-token prediction on these augmented sequences, with retrieved passage text excluded from the loss. At inference, each retrieved passage produces a candidate sentence continuation. Segment-level beam search combines generation probability with weighted, normalized probabilities for relevance, support, and utility tokens to choose continuations. This operation makes evidence quality part of selection rather than merely part of the prompt. Adjusting the retrieval threshold or critique weights changes behavior at test time without retraining or invoking the critic.

P08 / Results / Sample 3

Self-RAG: Learning to Retrieve, Generate, and Critique through Self-Reflection

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

158 words; within budget

On ASQA, Self-RAG 13B improves attribution without surpassing retrieval-augmented ChatGPT across the other reported objectives. Given the same five supplied passages, it reaches citation precision of 70.3 versus 65.1, but citation recall is lower, 71.3 versus 76.6, as is str-em answer correctness, 31.7 versus 40.7. Better-supported citations therefore coexist with less complete citation coverage and lower answer correctness. The PopQA control answers a different question: Self-RAG 7B achieves 54.9% accuracy versus 48.7% for Llama2-FT, which uses the same instruction-output pairs but receives retrieved passages only at inference. This comparison supports a benefit beyond example availability, while changing retrieval-aware training and reflection together rather than isolating either. The 7B customization analysis reveals another tradeoff: increasing the support weight raises citation precision but lowers MAUVE, while reducing retrieval degrades PopQA more than PubHealth. The controls thus expose a choice of operating point: prioritizing support or reducing retrieval can carry measurable costs elsewhere.

P09 / Abstract / Sample 1

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

175 words; within budget

ConvNeXt examines whether hierarchical vision Transformers' performance advantages require attention or can be recovered through convolutional design and modern training. Updating the training recipe alone raises ResNet-50 ImageNet-1K top-1 accuracy from 76.1% to 78.8%, establishing a stronger starting point for architectural comparisons. Under this fixed recipe, we progressively modify ResNet to separate spatial and channel mixing, placing large depthwise kernels before channel-expanding pointwise layers and simplifying activation and normalization patterns. The resulting convolutional hierarchy requires neither shifted-window attention nor relative position biases. With ImageNet-1K training at 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B reaches 85.1% accuracy versus Swin-B's 84.5%, alongside 12.5% higher inference throughput on a V100 GPU. ConvNeXt-XL reaches 87.8% top-1 accuracy at 384 x 384 resolution after ImageNet-22K pretraining and ImageNet-1K fine-tuning, extending the evaluation beyond the small-model exploration. COCO detection and instance segmentation and ADE20K semantic segmentation likewise match or exceed the evaluated Swin counterparts. These comparisons support modernized convolution as an alternative for the tested recognition tasks, without settling attention's usefulness for multimodal interaction or structured outputs.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: ConvNeXt progressively modernizes convolution under an improved fixed training recipe, separating spatial/channel mixing without specialized attention. ConvNeXt-B exceeds Swin-B accuracy with 12.5% higher throughput, supporting convolution as a tested recognition alternative.

Scope: ImageNet-1K, 384 x 384, V100: 85.1% versus 84.5%. ImageNet-22K pretraining/fine-tuning yields 87.8% for ConvNeXt-XL. COCO/ADE20K match or exceed evaluated Swin counterparts. Attention's usefulness for multimodal interaction or structured outputs remains unsettled.

Witness: without settling attention's usefulness for multimodal interaction or structured outputs

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P09 Sections 2-4 and Table 1 support training-first modernization, attention-free spatial/channel separation, B-model accuracy/12.5% V100 throughput advantage, and XL's 87.8 after IN-22K pretraining. Tables 3-4 support overall box/mask AP and mIoU competitiveness, not every AP submetric. Prose's multimodal/structured-output caveat is grounded in Section F and recovered by the reading. Named recognition workloads and unresolved alternative tasks adequately bound the conclusion.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P09 Sections 2-2.6 support training improvement followed by convolutional redesign. Table 1 supports 85.1% versus 84.5%, 12.5% greater V100 throughput, and pretrained XL 87.8%. Section 4 supports standard downstream competitiveness; F leaves multimodal/structured-output applicability open. The reader accurately recovers modern training plus design and explicitly retains the prose's task-specific conclusion and unsettled attention-use boundary.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

173 words; within budget

ConvNeXt investigates whether hierarchical vision Transformers' recognition performance can be matched by modernizing convolutional design and training without attention. A modern training recipe raises ResNet-50 ImageNet-1K top-1 accuracy from 76.1% to 78.8%, motivating an architectural comparison that keeps this recipe fixed. Sequential modifications separate spatial filtering from channel mixing: large depthwise kernels operate before channel expansion, allowing wider spatial coverage while concentrating dense computation in pointwise layers. The resulting convolutional hierarchy also simplifies activation and normalization patterns, without shifted-window attention or relative position biases. At 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B trained on ImageNet-1K reaches 85.1% top-1 accuracy versus Swin-B's 84.5%, with 12.5% higher inference throughput on a V100 GPU. Scaling data and capacity remains productive: after ImageNet-22K pretraining and ImageNet-1K fine-tuning at this resolution, ConvNeXt-XL reaches 87.8%. COCO detection and instance segmentation and ADE20K semantic segmentation yield performance comparable to or better than the evaluated Swin counterparts. The results show that competitive performance across these recognition tasks does not require the specialized attention modules used by Swin.

Shared initial draft

ConvNeXt examines how much of hierarchical vision Transformers' performance can be recovered without attention by updating convolutional design and training. We progressively modify ResNet under a fixed modern training recipe, combining depthwise spatial filtering, inverted bottlenecks, larger kernels, and simpler activation and normalization patterns. The resulting network separates spatial and channel mixing while retaining a conventional convolutional hierarchy, without shifted-window attention or relative position biases. Training changes alone raise ResNet-50 ImageNet-1K accuracy from 76.1% to 78.8%, showing that older training recipes account for part of the apparent architectural gap. At 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B trained on ImageNet-1K reaches 85.1% accuracy versus Swin-B's 84.5%, with 12.5% higher inference throughput on a V100 GPU. ImageNet-22K pretraining supports further scaling, with ConvNeXt-XL reaching 87.8% after ImageNet-1K fine-tuning. COCO detection and instance segmentation and ADE20K semantic segmentation also yield results comparable to or better than the evaluated Swin counterparts. These evaluations support modernized convolution as a competitive choice for the tested visual recognition tasks, while leaving attention's utility for multimodal and structured-output problems unresolved.

P09 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

140 words; within budget

ConvNeXt separates spatial filtering from channel mixing so that enlarging the receptive field does not require dense convolution across every channel pair. Each residual block applies a 7 x 7 depthwise convolution before pointwise layers expand the channel dimension fourfold and project it back. Keeping the spatial filter on the narrower representation limits its computational cost, while the dense channel transformations carry most of the work. A single GELU between the pointwise layers and one LayerNorm simplify the block. Separate stride-two convolutions downsample between stages; added normalization at resolution changes stabilizes this otherwise divergent design. These choices are developed through sequential modifications under a fixed 300-epoch AdamW recipe, with roughly controlled FLOPs and accuracy averaged over three seeds in the ResNet-50 regime. This controls the training recipe, but sequential changes and varying intermediate FLOPs do not isolate independent component effects.

P10 / Abstract / Sample 1

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

183 words; within budget

3D Gaussian splatting addresses the tension between optimizing a radiance field for image quality and rendering it quickly enough for interactive viewing. A static scene is represented by anisotropic 3D Gaussians initialized from sparse structure-from-motion points, with learnable geometry, opacity, and spherical-harmonic appearance. Because these primitives project directly to image-space splats, the same differentiable rasterizer supports reconstruction and subsequent viewing. Parameter updates alternate with cloning, splitting, and pruning to adapt the representation's density. The tile-based rasterizer shares sorting across pixels and blends splats in approximate depth order, without limiting how many contributing Gaussians receive gradients. Evaluation covers 13 real scenes and bounded synthetic scenes. At 30,000 iterations, real-scene dataset averages reach 134-154 frames per second on an A6000 GPU at their respective native resolutions, after approximately 27-42 minutes of training. The resulting quality exceeds that of the evaluated InstantNGP and Plenoxels configurations and is competitive with Mip-NeRF360, although the latter retains higher PSNR on its own dataset. This combination supports interactive novel-view synthesis, but stores larger models than InstantNGP and Mip-NeRF360 and retains artifacts under sparse view coverage or changing splat order.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Anisotropic 3D Gaussians with adaptive density and shared-sort differentiable rasterization unify reconstruction and interactive novel-view rendering without a gradient-contributor cap. Quality exceeds evaluated InstantNGP/Plenoxels configurations and competes with Mip-NeRF360, which retains higher PSNR on its dataset.

Scope: Static scenes initialized from sparse structure-from-motion. Thirteen real and bounded synthetic scenes; 30,000 iterations yield 134-154 fps on A6000 at native resolutions after 27-42 minutes. Models exceed InstantNGP/Mip-NeRF360 storage; sparse coverage and changing splat order produce artifacts.

Witness: blends splats in approximate depth order

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose 'respective native resolutions' and the reading preserve hardware/budget specificity and metric dependence. The quality statement concerns the reported aggregate configurations, not every synthetic object or individual view; static capture and artifact limits adequately bound the conclusion.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P10 Sections 3-6 support parameter learning, adaptive density, direct projection, and approximate blending without fixed gradient-count limits. P10 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

140 words; within budget

The Gaussian rasterizer shares visibility work across pixels to make the same explicit representation practical for optimization and interactive viewing. It partitions the screen into 16 x 16 tiles, duplicates each projected Gaussian across the tiles it overlaps, and sorts the instances once on the GPU using tile identifiers and depth. Tiles load splats into shared memory and blend them front to back until each pixel's opacity saturates. Shared ordering removes per-pixel sorting, but makes blending approximate, particularly for large splats. Backpropagation reuses the sorted tile lists in reverse, recovering intermediate blending quantities from accumulated opacity instead of storing variable-length lists for individual pixels. This permits gradients for every contributing splat without a predetermined count limit. Together, shared sorting and reverse traversal reduce repeated visibility work and storage overhead without truncating gradient propagation solely to keep a fixed splat count.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Duplicating projected Gaussians into overlapping tiles and sorting once shares visibility work across pixels. Forward blending and reverse traversal reconstruct blending quantities from accumulated opacity, reducing repeated sorting and per-pixel storage while retaining gradients for every contributor.

Scope: 16 x 16 tiles; shared-memory splat loading; blending ends at each pixel's opacity saturation. Shared depth order approximates visibility, particularly for large splats.

Witness: without a predetermined count limit

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose limits gradients to 'contributing' splats and retains opacity saturation and approximate large-splat ordering. The reading recovers the shared visibility/storage mechanism and these qualifications. The paragraph asserts reduced overhead without a count cap, not exact visibility or bounded total scene memory, so its selected scope is adequate.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. It explicitly acknowledges approximate ordering, increasingly negligible for small splats. The backward pass reuses the sorted tile lists in reverse, recovering intermediate blending quantities from the final accumulated opacity rather than storing each step. This connects rendering speed with an optimization requirement: reducing visibility-processing overhead while retaining updates through variable scene depth complexity.

Shared initial draft

The Gaussian rasterizer makes explicit primitives useful for both optimization and interactive viewing by sharing visibility work across pixels. It divides the screen into 16 x 16 tiles, duplicates each projected Gaussian for the tiles it overlaps, and performs one GPU radix sort using tile identifiers and depth. Each tile then loads batches of splats into shared memory and blends them from front to back, stopping a pixel when opacity saturates. Because ordering is shared rather than recomputed per pixel, blending is approximate, particularly for large splats. During backpropagation, the same sorted lists are traversed in reverse and intermediate blending quantities are recovered from accumulated opacity, avoiding variable-length per-pixel storage. Crucially, every contributing splat can receive a gradient without a predetermined count limit. This design reduces sorting and storage overhead while preserving updates through scenes with varying depth complexity.

P10 / Results / Sample 1

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

169 words; within budget

On the Mip-NeRF360 dataset, 30,000-iteration Gaussian splatting improves SSIM to 0.815 and LPIPS to 0.214 against the published reference values of 0.792 and 0.237, respectively, but has lower PSNR: 27.21 versus 27.69 dB. Competitive quality is therefore metric-dependent. Relative to 7,000 iterations, its dataset-average PSNR rises from 25.60 to 27.21 dB, while training increases from 6 minutes 25 seconds to 41 minutes 33 seconds and A6000 rendering falls from 160 to 134 frames per second at native test-image resolutions. Stored parameters grow from 523 to 734 MB, far above Mip-NeRF360's 8.6 MB. In the separate Truck ablation using manually downsampled inputs, limiting gradients to ten splats destabilizes optimization and reduces 30,000-iteration PSNR from 24.81 to 13.84 dB. This failure supports avoiding a fixed gradient cap in the tested optimization. The results support interactive rendering at competitive quality, with model storage and artifacts in poorly observed regions or during splat-order changes still limiting the evaluated system.

P09 / Abstract / Sample 2

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

172 words; within budget

ConvNeXt revisits the performance gap between conventional convolutional networks and hierarchical vision Transformers by updating training and architecture without introducing attention. Starting from ResNet, we first strengthen the training recipe and then hold it fixed while progressively changing the network at approximately controlled computational cost. The resulting design separates spatial and channel mixing: large depthwise kernels operate at a narrow width before pointwise layers expand and contract channels, making broader spatial processing affordable. Evaluations compare similarly sized Swin models on ImageNet classification, COCO detection and instance segmentation, and ADE20K semantic segmentation. At 384 x 384 resolution, ImageNet-1K-trained ConvNeXt-B achieves 85.1% top-1 accuracy against Swin-B's 84.5%, alongside V100 inference throughput of 95.7 versus 85.1 images per second. Scaling both model size and pretraining data yields 87.8% for ConvNeXt-XL after ImageNet-22K pretraining and ImageNet-1K fine-tuning at that resolution. Competitive downstream results show that the revised convolutional backbone is useful beyond classification. These results support a convolutional alternative for the evaluated recognition tasks without shifted-window attention or relative position biases.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: ConvNeXt strengthens ResNet training, then modernizes its convolutional architecture without attention. Narrow-width depthwise spatial kernels before pointwise expansion make broader filtering affordable; classification and downstream results support an alternative to Swin for evaluated recognition tasks.

Scope: Architectural steps fix training and approximately control cost. ImageNet-1K, 384 x 384: B achieves 85.1% versus Swin-B 84.5%, V100 throughput 95.7 versus 85.1 images/second. XL reaches 87.8% with ImageNet-22K pretraining; downstream covers COCO and ADE20K.

Witness: without shifted-window attention or relative position biases

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose 'for the evaluated recognition tasks' explicitly limits the conclusion. The reading recovers fixed training, approximate cost control, model/data scales, and named downstream datasets. This is sufficient scope for the selected attention-free alternative, without requiring the abstract to enumerate unevaluated task types.

P09 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

143 words; within budget

ConvNeXt makes large spatial kernels affordable by applying them before channel expansion. A 7 x 7 depthwise convolution mixes locations independently in each channel; two pointwise convolutions then expand the width fourfold and restore it, separating spatial processing from channel mixing. Keeping the depthwise operator at the narrow input width limits its cost, while the pointwise layers supply channel capacity. Each residual block retains one GELU and one layer normalization. Separate stride-two convolutions downsample between stages, but this change initially caused training to diverge; normalization at resolution transitions stabilized the revised design. Architectural changes are accumulated from a ResNet baseline under a fixed 300-epoch AdamW recipe with augmentation and regularization, retaining the same hyperparameters. FLOPs remain approximately controlled, and small-model accuracies average three training seeds. This sequence assesses architectural changes after the initial training gain, but each comparison remains conditional on earlier modifications.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: Residual blocks retain one GELU and one layer normalization. Sequential ResNet modifications use a fixed 300-epoch AdamW recipe, unchanged hyperparameters, approximately controlled FLOPs, and three-seed small-model averages. Comparisons depend on prior modifications.

Witness: each comparison remains conditional on earlier modifications

Unclear: Independent component effects are not recoverable.

Its inability to recover independent effects is the stated experimental limit, not failed recovery of the selected mechanism or scope.

P09 / Results / Sample 2

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

158 words; within budget

ConvNeXt-B's classification results show that retaining convolutions need not trade accuracy for throughput relative to Swin-B. With ImageNet-1K training and 384 x 384 inputs, top-1 accuracy is 85.1% versus 84.5%, and V100 inference throughput is 95.7 versus 85.1 images per second, at 45.0 versus 47.1 GFLOPs. This is not the best accuracy across all tested configurations: EfficientNetV2-L reaches 85.7%, with 480 x 480 inputs and 53.0 GFLOPs. The downstream comparisons test whether the redesigned backbone remains useful beyond classification. With ImageNet-22K pretraining and Cascade Mask R-CNN, ConvNeXt-B reaches 54.0 box AP and 46.9 mask AP against Swin-B's 53.0 and 45.8. Under the same pretraining regime, UperNet evaluation on ADE20K with 640 x 640 crops and multi-scale testing yields 53.1 versus 51.7 mIoU. The gains against Swin therefore extend to localization and dense prediction, even though the ImageNet-1K-trained EfficientNet model retains the higher classification score.

P10 / Abstract / Sample 2

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

165 words; within budget

Reconstructing a photographic scene for interactive viewing requires both tractable fitting and fast rendering. We represent static scenes as anisotropic 3D Gaussians initialized from sparse structure-from-motion points, with spherical harmonics encoding directional color. Their geometry can adapt during optimization, while direct projection into splats makes the representation accessible to a differentiable tile rasterizer. Cloning, splitting, and pruning redistribute primitives as fitting proceeds; shared depth sorting accelerates approximate opacity blending without imposing a fixed limit on gradient contributors. Evaluation covers 13 real scenes and synthetic bounded scenes. At 30,000 iterations, averages across the three real-scene datasets range from 134 to 154 frames per second at native image resolutions on an A6000, following roughly 27-42 minutes of training. Reconstruction quality is competitive with Mip-NeRF360, but not uniformly better: on the Mip-NeRF360 dataset, SSIM and LPIPS improve while PSNR remains lower. Interactive rendering is obtained at a substantial storage cost relative to neural radiance fields, with artifacts in poorly observed regions and occasional popping remaining practical limitations.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Adaptive anisotropic Gaussians with directional color and a differentiable tile rasterizer enable tractable scene fitting and interactive rendering. Shared depth sorting accelerates approximate blending while retaining unrestricted gradient contributors.

Scope: Static scenes initialized from sparse structure-from-motion points; 13 real and bounded synthetic scenes. At 30,000 iterations: 134-154 fps, native resolutions, A6000, roughly 27-42 minutes training. Mip-NeRF360 comparison favors SSIM/LPIPS but not PSNR on its dataset; storage costs, poorly observed-region artifacts, and popping persist.

Witness: Interactive rendering is obtained at a substantial storage cost relative to neural radiance fields

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose 'not uniformly better' and the reading preserve metric dependence. Static capture, iteration budget, resource cost, and poor coverage adequately bound the claimed interactive reconstruction.

Table 1 supports 134-154 FPS, roughly 27-42 minutes, and the mixed Mip-NeRF360 metrics; Section 7.2 supplies native resolutions/A6000, and Section 7.4 supplies storage/artifact limits. The reader accurately recovers the joint efficiency mechanism and these qualifications.

Shared initial draft

Photographic scene reconstruction must reconcile the cost of fitting a radiance field with the cost of rendering it for interactive exploration. We represent static scenes with optimized anisotropic 3D Gaussians, initialized from sparse structure-from-motion points and assigned spherical harmonic coefficients for directional color. Geometry and appearance are learned jointly while cloning, splitting, and pruning adapt the representation to missing detail and oversized primitives. A differentiable tile renderer sorts splats for approximate visibility-aware blending and propagates gradients without a fixed limit on contributors. Across 13 real scenes and synthetic bounded scenes, the method combines rapid rendering with progressively improved reconstruction. At 30,000 iterations, real-dataset averages reach 134-154 frames per second at native image resolutions on an A6000, after approximately 27-42 minutes of training. Quality is competitive with Mip-NeRF360, although on its dataset Gaussian splatting improves SSIM and LPIPS while retaining lower PSNR. The explicit representation therefore changes the practical quality-speed tradeoff rather than winning every image metric. Its costs include substantially larger stored models than neural radiance fields, artifacts in poorly observed regions, and occasional popping.

P10 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

140 words; within budget

The rasterizer avoids two costs that would make explicit splats expensive to train: sorting separately for each pixel and storing a variable-length history of blended contributors. It assigns projected Gaussians to 16 x 16 pixel tiles and sorts keys combining tile identity with view-space depth in one GPU radix operation. Pixels traverse the resulting tile lists from front to back, accumulating color and opacity until saturation. Backpropagation revisits those lists in reverse and recovers intermediate blending information from final accumulated opacity, avoiding individual contribution lists while retaining gradients for all contributing splats. This makes the rendering path usable for optimization at varying depth complexity, rather than accelerating display through a fixed gradient cutoff. Rendering losses update geometry, opacity, and spherical harmonic color alongside gradient-guided cloning and splitting. Because each tile shares one depth order across its pixels, blending remains approximate.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Tile/depth sorting replaces per-pixel sorting; reverse traversal reconstructs blending information from final opacity instead of storing contribution histories. This retains gradients for all contributors and makes splat rendering usable for optimization with varying depth complexity.

Scope: 16 x 16 tiles, one GPU radix sort, front-to-back blending until saturation. Losses update geometry, opacity, and spherical harmonic color with gradient-guided cloning and splitting. Shared tile ordering makes blending approximate.

Witness: rather than accelerating display through a fixed gradient cutoff

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P10 Section 6 supports tile/depth radix sorting, reverse traversal, saved final opacity, and gradients without a fixed contributor-count limit; Sections 4-5 support parameter and density updates. Prose 'until saturation' and 'blending remains approximate' bound the guarantee. The reading recovers the sorting/storage mechanism and both boundaries, not a guarantee of exact visibility or gradients for every scene primitive. The selected algorithmic scope is adequate.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P10 Section 6 describes 16 x 16 tiles, one tile/depth radix sort, opacity-saturation stopping, and reverse traversal recovering blending information without per-pixel histories or a fixed gradient-count cap. Section 5 supports parameter optimization and gradient-guided densification. The reader accurately recovers the prose's optimization rationale and explicitly retains approximate shared ordering.

The backward pass traverses the same lists in reverse, reconstructing intermediate opacity values from the stored final accumulation. Shared initial draft

To make an explicit radiance field trainable as well as fast to display, the renderer groups projected Gaussians into 16 x 16 pixel tiles. Each overlapping Gaussian receives a key combining tile identity and view-space depth, so a single GPU radix sort supplies ordered lists for all tiles. Pixels accumulate color and opacity from front to back, stopping when opacity saturates rather than after a fixed number of contributors. Rendering losses jointly update Gaussian geometry, opacity, and spherical harmonic color, interleaved with gradient-guided cloning and splitting. Ordering is shared within tiles rather than recomputed per pixel, so blending remains approximate.

P10 / Results / Sample 2

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

165 words; within budget

Real-time rendering does not require uniformly superior image metrics. On the Mip-NeRF360 dataset at 30,000 iterations, Gaussian splatting improves SSIM from 0.792 to 0.815 and LPIPS from 0.237 to 0.214 relative to the published Mip-NeRF360 scores, while PSNR is lower, at 27.21 versus 27.69 dB. It renders at 134 frames per second on an A6000 after an average 41 minutes 33 seconds of training, but stores 734 MB of parameters against the baseline's 8.6 MB. The practical gain is therefore interactive display at competitive quality, purchased with substantially greater storage. A separate Truck ablation tests whether fitting could be accelerated by limiting gradient contributors: at 30,000 iterations, restricting gradients to the ten frontmost splats lowers PSNR from 24.81 to 13.84 dB. This failure supports retaining gradients through the blended sequence rather than assuming a cutoff preserves quality. High frame rates nevertheless coexist with occasional popping and reconstruction artifacts where training views provide poor coverage.

P09 / Abstract / Sample 3

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

166 words; within budget

ConvNeXt tests whether the gains associated with hierarchical vision Transformers require attention or can be recovered through convolutional design and modern training. Starting from ResNet, the study fixes an enhanced training recipe and progressively transfers design choices from Swin into a convolutional backbone. The key block separates spatial and channel mixing: a 7 x 7 depthwise convolution acts before fourfold channel expansion, providing a larger spatial receptive field without applying the kernel at the expanded width. At 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B trained on ImageNet-1K reaches 85.1% top-1 accuracy versus 84.5% for Swin-B, with V100 inference throughput of 95.7 versus 85.1 images per second. ImageNet-22K pretraining followed by ImageNet-1K fine-tuning yields 87.8% top-1 accuracy for ConvNeXt-XL at the same resolution. Standard detection and segmentation heads yield competitive COCO and ADE20K results, extending the comparison beyond classification. The evidence establishes modernized convolution as a competitive alternative to Swin in these workloads, rather than identifying attention removal as an isolated cause of improvement.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: ConvNeXt transfers design choices into a convolutional backbone under fixed modern training. A narrow-width depthwise kernel precedes channel expansion, supporting higher classification accuracy and throughput than Swin-B without attention.

Scope: 7 x 7 kernel, fourfold expansion; ImageNet-1K training at 384 x 384, V100. ImageNet-22K pretraining yields 87.8% for ConvNeXt-XL. COCO/ADE20K results extend competitiveness beyond classification; attention removal is not isolated as the cause.

Witness: rather than identifying attention removal as an isolated cause of improvement

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Table 1 supports the B-model and XL numbers, resolution, and V100 throughput. Prose 'in these workloads' and 'rather than ... an isolated cause' limit task scope and causation; the reading recovers both. The selected recognition alternative is supported without asserting attention removal independently caused the gains.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P09 Sections 2-2.6 support transferring design choices under fixed modern training and narrow-width 7 x 7 filtering before fourfold expansion. Table 1 supports the B and pretrained XL results; Section 4 supports competitive standard-head downstream evaluation. The reader recovers the prose's convolutional alternative for these workloads and explicitly rejects isolated causal attribution to attention removal.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

161 words; within budget

Vision Transformers combine attention with training and architectural choices, leaving open how much of their recognition performance a convolutional network can recover. We investigate this question by progressively redesigning ResNet into ConvNeXt without introducing attention. After strengthening the training recipe, the study changes stage computation and separates spatial mixing from channel mixing: depthwise convolutions act on narrow features before pointwise layers expand and recombine channels. This arrangement accommodates larger spatial kernels, while normalized stage transitions stabilize the final network. At 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B trained on ImageNet-1K achieves 85.1% top-1 accuracy versus 84.5% for Swin-B and processes 95.7 versus 85.1 images per second on a V100 GPU. Scaling to ConvNeXt-XL with ImageNet-22K pretraining and ImageNet-1K fine-tuning yields 87.8% top-1 accuracy at the same resolution. Competitive results on COCO and ADE20K extend the comparison to detection and segmentation. Together, the redesign and evaluations show that competitive recognition backbones can incorporate Transformer-style organization without relying on self-attention.

Shared initial draft

Strong performance from vision Transformers need not imply that self-attention is the decisive ingredient for visual recognition. We investigate this distinction by progressively redesigning ResNet into ConvNeXt, a convolutional backbone that adopts hierarchical Transformer design choices without attention. The study first strengthens the training recipe, then examines computation across stages, spatial and channel mixing, and block-level normalization and activation. Depthwise spatial convolutions and expanded pointwise layers retain the separation of operations found in Transformer blocks, while normalized stage transitions stabilize the final architecture. At 384 x 384 resolution, ConvNeXt-B trained on ImageNet-1K achieves 85.1% top-1 accuracy, compared with 84.5% for Swin-B, and processes 95.7 rather than 85.1 images per second on a V100 GPU. ImageNet-22K pretraining followed by ImageNet-1K fine-tuning yields 87.8% for ConvNeXt-XL. Evaluations with standard detection and segmentation heads also show competitive performance on COCO and ADE20K. Together, these results support modernized convolution as an alternative to Swin for the tested recognition workloads and show that several useful Transformer design choices do not depend on attention.

P09 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

139 words; within budget

ConvNeXt applies its 7 x 7 depthwise convolution before channel expansion, so the larger spatial kernel operates at the block's narrower width. This mixes locations independently within each channel; the following 1 x 1 convolutions perform channel mixing, expanding width fourfold and then restoring it. Layer normalization precedes these pointwise layers, and a single GELU separates them within a residual block. The ordering keeps the larger spatial operation away from the expanded representation while preserving the separation of spatial and channel processing found in Transformer blocks. A nonoverlapping 4 x 4 stem and normalized, separate downsampling layers complete the hierarchy. The redesign is evaluated sequentially with a fixed 300-epoch AdamW recipe on ImageNet-1K and roughly controlled FLOPs; small-regime accuracies average three seeds. Thus, each comparison concerns a change conditional on the preceding architecture, rather than an independent component effect.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Sequential ImageNet-1K evaluation fixes 300-epoch AdamW training and roughly controls FLOPs; small-regime results average three seeds. Effects are conditional on preceding architecture.

Witness: rather than an independent component effect

Large spatial kernels become expensive when applied after a block expands its channels. ConvNeXt instead places a 7 x 7 depthwise convolution first, mixing spatial information separately within each channel at the narrower input width. Layer normalization then precedes two 1 x 1 convolutions that expand width fourfold and restore it, with one GELU between them and a residual connection around the block. P09 / Results / Sample 3

A ConvNet for the 2020s

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

161 words; within budget

An improved training recipe alone raises ResNet-50 accuracy from 76.1% to 78.8%, establishing why architecture should be assessed against the strengthened baseline. Within that fixed recipe, switching ReLU to GELU leaves top-1 accuracy at 80.6%, but reducing the block to one activation raises it to 81.3%. This contrast favors the tested activation layout over activation substitution alone. Separate downsampling initially causes training to diverge; normalization at the stem, stage transitions, and final pooling restores stable training, yielding 82.0% in the completed roadmap versus Swin-T's 81.3%. These gains remain conditional on preceding redesigns. A downstream comparison extends the finding beyond classification: with ImageNet-22K pretraining and Cascade Mask R-CNN under a 3x schedule, ConvNeXt-B obtains 54.0 box AP and 46.9 mask AP on COCO, against 53.0 and 45.8 for Swin-B. The COCO comparison supports transfer of the redesigned backbone, while the roadmap identifies combinations that work and substitutions that do not improve accuracy.

P10 / Abstract / Sample 3

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

172 words; within budget

Interactive exploration of captured scenes requires both fast rendering and sufficient reconstruction quality, a combination not provided by training speed alone. The method represents a static scene with anisotropic 3D Gaussians initialized from sparse structure-from-motion points, avoiding normal estimation while allowing geometry, opacity, and directional appearance to be optimized. Cloning and splitting refine the primitive allocation, while a tile-based renderer shares a depth ordering across pixels instead of sorting each pixel separately and reconstructs opacity information during backpropagation. This combination supports fast splatting without imposing a fixed limit on the number of blended primitives that receive gradients. Across held-out views from three real-scene datasets, the 30,000-iteration configuration renders at dataset-average rates of 134-154 frames per second on an A6000 after approximately 27-42 minutes of training. Relative to Mip-NeRF360, it has higher SSIM and lower LPIPS on all three datasets, but lower PSNR on the Mip-NeRF360 dataset. The speed-quality tradeoff requires hundreds of megabytes of stored parameters, with residual artifacts in weakly observed regions and occasional popping associated with approximate visibility handling.

P10 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

142 words; within budget

Efficient splatting requires visibility ordering, but sorting and storing a separate contribution history for every pixel would be costly. The renderer projects each Gaussian to an image-space footprint and duplicates it across overlapping 16 x 16 tiles. Keys combining tile identity and view-space depth permit one GPU radix sort to organize all tile lists. Pixels blend these lists front to back until opacity saturates; sharing the order avoids per-pixel sorting, at the cost of approximate visibility. The backward pass reuses the lists in reverse and reconstructs intermediate opacity factors from saved final accumulations instead of retaining variable-length histories. This keeps the extra per-pixel storage constant while allowing gradients for every blended primitive, without a fixed count cutoff. Optimization updates geometry, opacity, and spherical-harmonic appearance using L1 plus D-SSIM loss, with positional-gradient thresholds triggering cloning of small Gaussians or splitting of large ones.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: 16 x 16 tiles; blending stops at opacity saturation and shared ordering approximates visibility. L1 plus D-SSIM updates geometry, opacity, and appearance; positional gradients trigger cloning or splitting.

Witness: without a fixed count cutoff

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

The reading recovers the sorting/storage relationship plus tile size, saturation, and approximate visibility; this selected method scope does not require benchmark protocols.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

139 words; within budget

Sorting overlapping splats separately at every pixel and storing each pixel's blending history would make differentiable rendering costly. The renderer projects Gaussian covariances into image space, assigns splat instances to 16 x 16 pixel tiles, and uses keys combining tile identity and view-space depth for one GPU radix sort. Each tile reuses the resulting ordered list while its pixels blend front to back until opacity saturation. This avoids per-pixel sorting, although the shared order approximates visibility. During backpropagation, reverse traversal of the same lists reconstructs intermediate opacity factors from saved final accumulations, avoiding variable-length per-pixel histories without imposing a fixed primitive-count limit on gradients. A combined L1 and D-SSIM image loss updates geometry, opacity, and appearance. Between updates, positional gradients select small Gaussians for cloning or large ones for splitting, allowing sparse initial coverage to acquire additional geometric detail.

Shared initial draft

Rendering projects each Gaussian's covariance into image space and assigns its footprint to 16 x 16 pixel tiles. Gaussian instances receive keys combining tile identity and view-space depth, enabling one GPU radix sort to prepare all tile lists. Threads then blend colors and opacity front to back, stopping at opacity saturation. Avoiding per-pixel sorting makes anisotropic splatting efficient, but the shared depth order is an approximation. For differentiation, the renderer traverses those lists backward and reconstructs intermediate opacity factors from saved accumulations, rather than storing a variable-length history for every pixel. No fixed primitive-count cutoff is imposed on gradients. The image loss combines L1 error with D-SSIM and updates Gaussian geometry, opacity, and appearance. Interleaved density control clones small Gaussians or splits large ones when positional gradients indicate unresolved reconstruction, so the sparse initialization can gain detail as optimization proceeds.

P10 / Results / Sample 3

3D Gaussian Splatting for Real-Time Radiance Field Rendering

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

153 words; within budget

Longer optimization improves reconstruction but changes the deployment cost. On the Mip-NeRF360 dataset, moving from 7,000 to 30,000 iterations raises PSNR from 25.60 to 27.21 dB and reduces LPIPS from 0.279 to 0.214; rendering drops from 160 to 134 frames per second and stored parameters increase from 523 to 734 MB. The longer-trained model has higher SSIM and lower LPIPS than Mip-NeRF360 on this dataset, yet falls below its 27.69 dB PSNR, so the quality comparison remains metric-dependent. A separate ablation exposes the cost of restricting gradients. With manually downsampled inputs for Truck, Garden, and Bicycle, updating only the ten frontmost Gaussians yields 19.19 dB average PSNR after 30,000 iterations, versus 26.05 dB for the full method, and causes unstable optimization. Preserving gradients is therefore consequential in these scenes, although the full method still leaves artifacts under weak coverage and occasional visibility-related popping.

P11 / Abstract / Sample 1

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

186 words; within budget

Prediction sets can incur graded costs even when they contain a correct label, so binary coverage control does not directly regulate many application losses. Conformal risk control calibrates a prediction family to a target expected loss by reserving a worst-case contribution for one unseen observation in the calibration mean. For exchangeable, bounded, non-increasing, right-continuous loss functions with terminal loss at most the target, this correction keeps expected test loss at most the target. If the losses are additionally independent, identically distributed, nonnegative, and have zero probability of a jump at any fixed parameter, the shortfall is at most 2B/(n+1), where B bounds loss and n is the calibration size. This factor is tight in general, characterizing the procedure's worst-case conservatism. Monotonicity is consequential: bounded non-monotone losses can produce expected risk arbitrarily close to B. Replacing them with monotone upper envelopes restores control but may be conservative. Segmentation, multilabel classification, hierarchical classification, and question answering yield mean risks near their respective targets over 1,000 random splits. These results extend finite-sample calibration beyond indicator errors while identifying the loss structure on which its guarantee depends.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Conformal risk control extends binary coverage calibration to graded expected loss by reserving an unseen observation's worst-case contribution; expected test loss remains at most target.

Scope: Requires exchangeable, bounded, non-increasing, right-continuous losses and a safe terminal. IID nonnegative losses without fixed-parameter jumps have tight shortfall bound 2B/(n+1). Nonmonotone bounded losses can approach B; upper envelopes restore control but may be conservative. Four applications average near target across 1,000 splits.

Witness: identifying the loss structure on which its guarantee depends.

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P11 Sections 2.1-2.3 support the corrected expected-loss guarantee, sharp 2B/(n+1) shortfall under stronger conditions and conservative envelope repair. Section 3 supports near-target means over 1,000 splits in four applications. Prose lists exchangeability, non-increasing/right-continuous losses and a safe terminal, then separately states tightness assumptions. The reading recovers this selected generalization and its structural scope, rather than treating boundedness or the examples as sufficient for arbitrary losses.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P11 Theorems 1-2 and Proposition 1 support corrected expected-loss calibration and tight worst-case conservatism; Proposition 2 and Corollary 1 support non-monotone failure and envelope conservatism. Section 3 supplies the four application means across 1,000 splits. Reader preserves the unseen-observation relation and separates base assumptions from stronger tightness assumptions; no absent-source conditional guarantee is inferred.

Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

183 words; within budget

Binary coverage errors do not capture graded costs such as the fraction of tumor pixels missed. Conformal risk control calibrates expected loss to a target alpha using a corrected calibration mean that reserves the worst-case loss of one unseen observation. For exchangeable losses bounded above by B, non-increasing and right-continuous in the prediction parameter, with terminal losses at most alpha, the selected parameter guarantees expected test loss at most alpha. With independent, identically distributed nonnegative losses and zero probability of a jump at any fixed parameter, expected risk falls no more than 2B/(n+1) below the target; this bound is tight, with n the calibration size. Segmentation, multilabel classification, hierarchical classification and question answering yield mean risks near their respective targets over 1,000 random splits. Further constructions control quantiles and multiple risks, and accommodate distribution shifts with specified weighting information. The limitation is substantive: bounded non-monotone losses can yield expected test risk arbitrarily close to B despite a smaller target. Monotone upper envelopes recover control but may be conservative, making the loss's dependence on the prediction parameter central to the guarantee.
