Shared initial draft

Prediction sets can incur graded costs even when they contain a correct label, so controlling binary coverage error does not directly regulate many application losses. Conformal risk control calibrates a prediction family to a user-specified expected loss by adding a finite-sample correction to its calibration mean. For exchangeable, bounded, non-increasing, right-continuous loss functions with a feasible terminal parameter, the selected parameter keeps expected loss on a new observation at most the target. For independent, identically distributed nonnegative losses with zero probability of a jump at any fixed parameter, the shortfall from the target is at most 2B/(n+1), where B bounds loss and n is the calibration size; this bound is tight. Monotonicity is consequential: bounded non-monotone losses can yield expected risk arbitrarily close to their upper bound. Replacing them with monotone upper envelopes restores control but may be conservative. Applications to segmentation, multilabel classification, hierarchical classification, and question answering produce mean risks near their targets across 1,000 random splits. Further constructions address quantiles, multiple risks, and distribution shifts with specified weighting information, extending calibration beyond indicator errors while preserving the assumptions needed for each guarantee.

P11 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

150 words; within budget

Conformal risk control selects the smallest parameter lambda satisfying (n R_n(lambda) + B)/(n+1) <= alpha, where R_n is the mean calibration loss, n the calibration size, B a common upper bound on loss, and alpha the target. If none satisfies the constraint, it uses the maximal parameter, whose loss must already be at most alpha. The B term accounts for an unseen observation: the corrected mean upper-bounds the average of all n+1 losses, including the test loss. The proof compares the selected parameter with an oracle calibrated on that full sample. Because the correction is more conservative, the selected parameter is no smaller; non-increasing losses therefore make its test loss no larger. Conditional on the unordered loss functions, exchangeability makes the test function uniformly representative of the collection. With right-continuity ensuring the oracle attains its constraint, this comparison converts an empirical calibration rule into a finite-sample expected-risk guarantee.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

The B correction upper-bounds the full calibration-plus-test average; comparison with its oracle and non-increasing losses ensures no larger test loss, converting calibration into finite-sample expected-risk control.

Scope: Requires a common upper bound and maximal-parameter loss at most alpha. Exchangeability makes the held-out test function representative conditional on the unordered collection; right-continuity ensures oracle constraint attainment.

Witness: this comparison converts an empirical calibration rule into a finite-sample expected-risk guarantee

Unclear: none

The reading recovers the correction-to-oracle relationship, and its combined core/scope includes the necessary loss and endpoint conditions. It does not mistake conditioning on the unordered full sample within the proof for a guarantee conditional on a fitted calibration sample.

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

171 words; within budget

The false-negative experiments place average risk near the requested target while retaining appreciable variation between calibration splits. At alpha = 0.1, polyp segmentation used 1,000 calibration images and 781 evaluation images, yielding a mean risk of 0.0987 and a standard deviation of 0.0114 over 1,000 random splits. MS COCO multilabel classification used 4,000 calibration and 1,000 evaluation images and yielded 0.0996 with a standard deviation of 0.0052 under the same target and repetition count. The means are consistent with expected-risk control; the dispersion matters because the theorem does not cap risk separately for every split. MS COCO's smaller spread cannot be attributed to its larger calibration set alone, since the tasks and models differ. The theoretical non-monotone counterexample supplies a sharper boundary: expected risk can approach the upper loss bound B despite a lower target. Thus, near-target means in the monotone examples do not establish control for arbitrary bounded losses. An upper-envelope replacement restores the guarantee, with conservatism governed by departures from monotonicity.

P12 / Abstract / Sample 1

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

178 words; within budget

Updating a prediction model and its calibration scores does not necessarily preserve coverage when the data distribution shifts. Adaptive conformal inference instead uses observed coverage errors to adjust the quantile's miscoverage argument: a miss requests wider sets, while a covered response permits narrower sets. With immediate response feedback and the prescribed quantile endpoint extensions, empirical miscoverage converges to its target without assumptions on the data-generating distribution. This controls accumulated errors, not coverage at every time. Stronger concentration and average squared marginal-error bounds require stationary initialization in a hidden Markov model with fixed score and quantile functions and additional regularity conditions. These bounds connect performance to changes in the parameter that would attain the target in each environment. Stock-volatility tests and a county-vote experiment with constructed east-to-west ordering show local coverage nearer 90% than fixed-parameter calibration. However, removing volatility-score normalization produces wider coverage fluctuations even with adaptation. Coverage feedback therefore complements score design rather than replacing it. The step size also matters: faster updates respond more readily to shifts but increase parameter volatility, while weak adaptation approaches fixed-parameter calibration.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Coverage-error feedback requests wider sets after misses and narrower sets after coverage, restoring target long-run miscoverage despite distribution shifts. Stock and constructed county experiments show local improvements; score normalization remains consequential.

Scope: Requires immediate feedback and endpoint extensions; not time-point coverage. Stronger bounds assume stationary hidden Markov initialization and fixed score/quantile functions, relating error to target-attaining parameter changes. Larger steps trade responsiveness for volatility; weaker adaptation approaches fixed calibration.

Witness: Coverage feedback therefore complements score design rather than replacing it.

Unclear: Additional regularity conditions are not recoverable.

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose 'not coverage at every time' and 'constructed east-to-west ordering' adequately limit theory and experiments. The reading recovers these distinctions and the step-size tradeoff. Additional regularity remains acknowledged rather than reconstructed; the paragraph does not promise the stronger bounds without it.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P12 Sections 2 and 4.1 support immediate-feedback long-run frequency; Section 4.2 supports stationary hidden Markov, fixed-function sharper theory. Sections 2.1, 2.2, 5, and 6 support step-size behavior, local comparisons, constructed ordering, and normalization sensitivity. Reader recovers feedback plus accumulated-versus-per-time scope and appropriately does not fill in the paragraph's unnamed additional regularity conditions.

P12 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 1

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

144 words; within budget

Adaptive conformal inference separates target miscoverage alpha from alpha_t, which selects the 1 - alpha_t quantile of fitted conformity scores. After observing the response, it updates alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha - err_t), where err_t indicates a miss and gamma > 0 is the step size. A miss lowers alpha_t, raising the quantile threshold and requesting wider sets; coverage reverses that adjustment. Extending a non-decreasing quantile function outside [0,1] to yield full or empty sets keeps alpha_t within [-gamma, 1+gamma] when alpha_1 lies in [0,1]. Summing the updates yields average(err_t) - alpha = (alpha_1 - alpha_{T+1})/(gamma T). This identity turns bounded parameter movement into distribution-free control of accumulated errors, without estimating the shift. The stronger marginal-error analysis additionally assumes stationary initialization in a hidden Markov model, fixed score and quantile functions, Lipschitz statewise miscoverage, and an interior target-attaining parameter in every state.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: Requires gamma>0, alpha_1 in [0,1], and non-decreasing quantiles extended to full/empty sets. Stronger marginal-error analysis assumes stationary hidden Markov initialization, fixed score/quantile functions, Lipschitz statewise miscoverage, and an interior target-attaining parameter in every state.

Witness: without estimating the shift

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

The reading recovers the feedback-to-accumulated-error relationship and these boundaries. It does not mistake the stronger marginal result for an assumption-free guarantee.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P12 Section 2 supports the distinction between target miscoverage and its adaptive quantile argument. The extracted Lemma 4.1 interval loses symbols, limiting direct notation verification rather than establishing an erroneous bound.

P11 / Abstract / Sample 2

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

182 words; within budget

Conformal prediction addresses whether a set contains the target, whereas segmentation and other tasks also require control of graded errors. Conformal risk control extends calibration to expected loss by reserving a worst-case contribution for the unseen observation before choosing among nested prediction sets. For n calibration observations, the rule bounds expected test loss by alpha when the losses are exchangeable, non-increasing, right-continuous, bounded above by B, and admit a safe maximal parameter. This guarantee averages over calibration and test data rather than applying to each realized split. If the losses are also independent, identically distributed, and nonnegative, with no discontinuity at any fixed parameter value almost surely, expected loss is at least alpha - 2B/(n+1); the gap is sharp for general monotone losses. Monotonicity is consequential: bounded non-monotone losses can make the same rule incur expected loss arbitrarily close to B. Applications to segmentation, multilabel classification, hierarchical classification, and question answering produce average risks near their targets over 1,000 random splits. Together, the bounds and examples establish calibration beyond binary coverage while showing why boundedness alone cannot ensure risk control.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Conformal risk control extends binary coverage to graded expected-loss control by reserving B for an unseen observation; expected test loss is at most alpha.

Scope: Requires exchangeability, monotonicity, right-continuity, boundedness and a safe maximum; averages over calibration/test randomness. IID nonnegative losses without fixed-parameter jumps additionally give the sharp lower bound alpha-2B/(n+1). Without monotonicity risk can approach B. Four applications average near target across 1,000 splits.

Witness: boundedness alone cannot ensure risk control.

Unclear: none

Prose 'rather than applying to each realized split' and the distinct i.i.d./nonnegative/no-fixed-jump conditions adequately delimit the guarantees. The reading recovers this generalization, averaging scope and monotonicity boundary. The selected abstract does not need every extension or repair mechanism to remain faithful.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. The fixed-parameter discontinuity wording does not assert globally continuous sample paths. Section 3 and Proposition 2 support application means and bounded non-monotone failure. Reader preserves both assumption tiers and the explicit calibration/test-average limitation, rather than inferring splitwise control.

P11 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

141 words; within budget

For n calibration observations, write R_n(lambda) for the mean loss and let B bound each loss from above. The rule takes the infimum of parameters satisfying [n R_n(lambda) + B]/(n+1) <= alpha, using the safe maximal parameter if none qualifies. Reserving B for the unseen case makes the corrected calibration average dominate the average that would include its actual loss. The proof compares this implementable choice with an infeasible threshold computed symmetrically from all n+1 loss functions. The corrected choice is at least as large, so non-increasing losses give it no greater test loss. Conditional on the unordered collection, exchangeability makes the expected held-out loss equal the full-sample average at the symmetric threshold; right-continuity ensures that threshold achieves the target. Thus the correction controls expected loss over calibration and test randomness without requiring the test outcome during calibration.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: Requires an upper loss bound, non-increasing losses, exchangeability and right-continuity. The guarantee averages over both calibration and test randomness rather than treating the calibration mean as test risk.

Witness: without requiring the test outcome during calibration

P12 / Abstract / Sample 2

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

188 words; within budget

Refitting a prediction model and its calibration scores does not necessarily preserve coverage under distribution shift. Adaptive conformal inference adjusts the nominal miscoverage parameter after each revealed outcome: a miss lowers it to favor larger subsequent sets, while coverage raises it. Calibrating the quantile argument in this way avoids specifying a model of the shift. With a positive fixed step size and quantile conventions allowing whole-space or empty sets, long-run miscoverage frequency converges to the target for any data sequence. This guarantee does not establish coverage at each time. A hidden Markov analysis, with joint stationarity of the environment and adaptive parameter, fixed score and quantile functions, and additional regularity assumptions, provides concentration and mean squared marginal miscoverage bounds that depend on environmental persistence and shifts in the optimal calibration parameter. Volatility forecasting and county-level election prediction show improved local coverage relative to fixed nominal calibration. However, using unnormalized volatility residuals produces much wider coverage swings despite adaptation. The experiments therefore qualify the reach of feedback: local performance depends on score design, while the step size trades responsiveness against stability. The method requires an outcome at every step.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Scope: Needs positive fixed step size and full/empty-set quantiles, not per-time coverage. Hidden Markov concentration/squared marginal-error bounds require joint stationarity, fixed scores/quantiles and additional regularity. Unnormalized volatility scores worsen swings; step size trades responsiveness for stability; each outcome is required.

Witness: This guarantee does not establish coverage at each time.

P12 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 2

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

135 words; within budget

At time t, form the prediction set from responses whose current score is at most Q_t(1-alpha_t). After observing Y_t, encode a miss as err_t = 1 and coverage as err_t = 0, then update alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha-err_t). A miss lowers the parameter to favor larger sets; coverage raises it. This operation calibrates the quantile argument and can accompany updates to the score and quantile functions themselves. With gamma > 0, alpha_1 in [0,1], and out-of-range quantile conventions producing whole-space or empty sets, alpha_t remains in [-gamma,1+gamma] without clipping. Summing the recursion expresses cumulative excess miscoverage as (alpha_1-alpha_{T+1})/gamma. Boundedness therefore limits absolute frequency deviation to (max{alpha_1,1-alpha_1}+gamma)/(gamma T), regardless of the data-generating process. This explains the long-run guarantee, but not accurate coverage within every short window.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Form sets at Q_t(1-alpha_t), then update alpha_{t+1}=alpha_t+gamma(alpha-err_t) after observing Y_t. Misses favor wider sets; coverage raises the parameter. Telescoping bounds frequency deviation by (max{alpha_1,1-alpha_1}+gamma)/(gamma T), regardless of the data process.

Scope: Requires gamma>0, alpha_1 in [0,1] and whole-space/empty-set quantile conventions; alpha_t stays in [-gamma,1+gamma] without clipping. Score/quantile functions may also update. Long-run control does not imply accuracy within each short window.

Witness: This operation calibrates the quantile argument

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P12 Sections 2 and 4.1 support simultaneous score/quantile updates, error-feedback calibration, the telescoping cumulative identity and exact finite-horizon frequency bound. Candidate Primary: pass; text-only: pass

142 words; within budget

For target miscoverage alpha, threshold the current score at its estimated quantile Q_t(1-alpha_t). Once the response is revealed, set err_t to one for a miss and zero otherwise, then update alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha-err_t). A miss raises the requested quantile level, enlarging the set when the score and quantile functions are held fixed. For gamma > 0 and alpha_1 in [0,1], conventions producing full sets when alpha_t < 0 and empty sets when alpha_t > 1 keep alpha_t in [-gamma,1+gamma] without clipping. Summing the recursion gives (1/T) sum_{t=1}^T err_t - alpha = (alpha_1-alpha_{T+1})/(gamma T). The absolute frequency deviation is therefore at most [max{alpha_1,1-alpha_1}+gamma]/(gamma T), irrespective of the data-generating sequence. The guarantee follows from bounded feedback, not a distributional model, and concerns accumulated errors rather than marginal coverage at every time.

Shared initial draft

At time t, the method forms a prediction set by thresholding the current conformity score at the estimated quantile indexed by 1-alpha_t. After observing the response, it records err_t as one for a miss and zero otherwise, then applies alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha-err_t), with gamma > 0. A miss therefore enlarges subsequent sets by decreasing the nominal miscoverage parameter; a covered response moves it in the opposite direction. Quantile conventions outside the unit interval allow the whole response space or an empty set, keeping the parameter bounded even though it is not clipped to [0,1]. Boundedness consequently limits absolute frequency deviation to (max{alpha_1,1-alpha_1}+gamma)/(gamma T), yielding convergence without a distributional model for the observations.

P11 / Abstract / Sample 3

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

181 words; within budget

Prediction-set calibration can target the severity of errors, not just whether the correct answer is included. Conformal risk control selects a parameter after correcting the average of n calibration losses for the worst possible loss on an unseen observation. For exchangeable, non-increasing, right-continuous loss functions bounded above by B and with an endpoint loss at most the target alpha, this rule guarantees expected test loss at most alpha. If the functions are also independent and identically distributed, nonnegative, and have zero probability of a jump at any fixed parameter, expected loss is at least alpha - 2B/(n + 1); examples show this general lower bound is sharp. Segmentation, multilabel and hierarchical classification, and question answering illustrate control of nonbinary losses over 1,000 random data splits. The guarantee averages over calibration and test data, rather than certifying risk for every fitted set. Monotonicity supplies the link between parameter selection and risk control: without it, expected loss can approach B despite a lower target. A monotone upper envelope restores control but can yield conservative sets when the original loss is far from monotone.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Worst-case unseen-loss correction extends calibration from binary coverage to expected error severity, guaranteeing loss at most alpha. Monotonicity links selected parameters to control; an upper envelope repairs non-monotone losses but can be conservative.

Scope: Requires exchangeable, non-increasing, right-continuous losses bounded by B and a safe endpoint. The sharp lower bound alpha-2B/(n+1) additionally requires i.i.d., nonnegative losses without fixed-parameter jumps. Control averages calibration/test randomness, not every fitted set; non-monotone risk can approach B. Four application types use 1,000 splits.

Witness: rather than certifying risk for every fitted set

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Primary audit: P11 Sections 2.1-2.3 support unseen-loss correction, the sharp lower bound under additional assumptions and non-monotone failure/repair; Section 3 supports the four nonbinary applications and split count. Prose 'rather than certifying risk for every fitted set' and its separately stated tightness conditions adequately delimit the guarantee. The reading recovers graded expected-loss calibration, the monotonicity link and conservative envelope repair without claiming individual-set or unconditional tightness guarantees.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P11 Theorem 1 supports unseen-loss correction and expected-risk control; Theorem 2 and Proposition 1 support the stronger sharp lower bound. Section 3 supports four application types, and Proposition 2/Corollary 1 support non-monotone failure and potentially conservative envelopes. Reader preserves both assumption tiers and the explicit calibration/test-average versus every-fitted-set distinction.

Shared initial draft

Prediction sets can be calibrated for graded errors, not only for whether they contain the correct label. Conformal risk control chooses a parameter from calibration losses after adding a finite-sample correction that accounts for an unseen observation. P11 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

Conformal Risk Control

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

135 words; within budget

With n calibration loss functions and upper bound B, conformal risk control chooses the smallest parameter lambda satisfying [n R_n(lambda) + B]/(n + 1) <= alpha, where R_n is their average. If none qualifies, it uses the maximum parameter, whose loss must already be at most alpha. The correction reserves room for an unseen loss as large as B instead of treating the calibration average as the test risk. The proof introduces an oracle that applies the target to the average of all n + 1 losses. The implementable choice is at least as large as the oracle's; non-increasing losses therefore make it no riskier. Conditional on the multiset of loss functions, exchangeability identifies the oracle's expected test loss with that full average, and right-continuity ensures the threshold is attained. Together, these steps establish finite-sample expected-risk control.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Choose the smallest lambda satisfying [n R_n(lambda)+B]/(n+1)<=alpha, falling back to a safe maximum. Reserving B for unseen loss makes this choice at least as large as a full-sample oracle's; non-increasing losses make it no riskier, establishing finite-sample expected-risk control.

Scope: Requires a common upper loss bound and a maximal parameter whose loss is at most alpha. Exchangeability links oracle test loss to its full average; right-continuity ensures threshold attainment.

Witness: The correction reserves room for an unseen loss as large as B

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

The selected proof scope is adequate, without promising calibration-conditional control.

139 words; within budget

Simply thresholding mean calibration loss leaves the unseen test loss unaccounted for. Conformal risk control instead defines R_n(lambda) as the mean of n calibration losses bounded above by B and selects the smallest parameter satisfying [n R_n(lambda) + B]/(n + 1) <= alpha. If no parameter qualifies, it uses an endpoint whose loss is at most alpha. The correction upper-bounds the average that would include the test observation. An oracle using all n + 1 losses would therefore select a parameter no larger than the implementable choice. Conditional on their unordered collection, exchangeability makes the oracle's expected test loss equal their average; right-continuity ensures that average meets the target. Since the losses are nonincreasing, the implementable parameter cannot increase loss relative to the oracle. The empirical rule thus inherits a finite-sample expectation guarantee rather than relying on calibrated model probabilities.

Shared initial draft

Calibration replaces the unknown test risk with the average of n observed loss functions, but does not threshold that average alone. It selects the smallest parameter satisfying [n R_n(lambda) + B]/(n + 1) <= alpha, using the maximum parameter if no value qualifies, provided that endpoint itself has loss at most alpha. The additional B term treats the unseen loss at its worst allowed value. To justify this operation, the proof compares the selected parameter with an oracle computed from all n + 1 losses. P12 / Abstract / Sample 3

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

169 words; within budget

Refitting a prediction model and its score quantiles does not necessarily restore conformal coverage when the data distribution changes. Adaptive conformal inference updates a miscoverage parameter from each newly observed coverage error: a miss lowers the parameter to favor larger sets, whereas a covered response reverses the adjustment. Summing this feedback recursion turns cumulative miscoverage error into a difference between bounded calibration levels, yielding a distribution-free finite-horizon bound and convergence of long-run empirical miscoverage to the target. This controls accumulated errors, not coverage at every time point. A hidden Markov analysis also provides concentration and approximate marginal-coverage bounds with fixed scores and quantiles, stationary initialization, and additional regularity conditions. Stock-volatility forecasting and time-zone-ordered county-vote prediction show smaller local coverage departures than fixed-level calibration. However, replacing normalized volatility scores with unnormalized absolute errors produces much wider swings, revealing that adaptation does not remove sensitivity to score design. The step size balances responsiveness to shifting calibration requirements against volatility in the parameter, and updates require a response at each time point.

Shared initial draft

Nonstationary data can make a fixed conformal calibration level yield the wrong coverage even when prediction models and score quantiles are repeatedly refitted. Adaptive conformal inference adjusts that level online using the latest observed coverage error: a miss shifts calibration toward larger sets, whereas a covered response shifts it toward smaller ones. The feedback recursion yields a distribution-free guarantee that the long-run empirical miscoverage frequency converges to the chosen target, with a finite-horizon discrepancy bound. This guarantee does not establish target coverage at each time point. Stronger concentration and approximate marginal-coverage results are derived in a hidden Markov setting with fixed score and quantile functions and stationary initialization, with additional regularity conditions. Stock-volatility forecasting and time-zone-ordered county vote prediction illustrate how adaptation can reduce local coverage departures relative to a fixed level. However, volatility experiments with unnormalized scores exhibit substantially wider coverage swings, showing that feedback does not eliminate dependence on score design. The step size balances responsiveness against instability, and the procedure requires each response to be observed before the next update.

P12 / Methods / Analysis / Sample 3

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

130 words; within budget

Let alpha be the target miscoverage rate and alpha_t the parameter specifying the score threshold Q_t(1 - alpha_t). After the response is observed, update alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha - err_t), where err_t indicates a miss and gamma > 0. A miss lowers alpha_t, raising the threshold for a fixed quantile function; a covered response reverses this adjustment. Summing the recursion gives average miscoverage minus alpha as (alpha_1 - alpha_{T+1})/(gamma T). With alpha_1 in [0, 1], non-decreasing quantiles extended to yield full or empty sets outside their probability range keep alpha_t in [-gamma, 1 + gamma]. Bounded calibration levels therefore make the discrepancy vanish as T grows, without specifying a model of the shift. This guarantee concerns accumulated errors, however: extreme sets are permitted, and short-window coverage need not follow.

Reader reconstruction and source audit

Core: Update alpha_{t+1}=alpha_t+gamma(alpha-err_t) after each response; misses lower alpha_t and raise a fixed quantile threshold. Summation makes average excess miscoverage equal (alpha_1-alpha_{T+1})/(gamma T), so bounded parameters yield long-run target frequency without modeling shift.

Scope: Requires gamma>0, initialization in [0,1] and non-decreasing quantiles extended to full/empty sets, bounding alpha_t in [-gamma,1+gamma]. Extreme sets are allowed; individual or short-window coverage is not guaranteed.

Witness: short-window coverage need not follow.

Unclear: none

Recovery scores: core 2/2; scope 2/2.

Prose specifies initialization, positive step size and non-decreasing quantiles extended to full/empty sets, then says 'short-window coverage need not follow.' The reading recovers both the bounded-feedback mechanism and these boundaries, including permitted extreme sets. No shift model or pointwise guarantee is inferred.

Supplementary text-only audit: pass; core 2/2; scope 2/2. P12 Section 2's feedback description and Section 4.1 support the telescoping argument and accumulated-error guarantee. Prose and reader explicitly retain initialization, full/empty extensions, positive step size, and the short-window limitation. Section 4.1's extracted endpoint and step-size symbols are damaged, so their exact printed notation cannot be verified; the feedback direction and bounded-iterate proof support the stated relation, not a demonstrated numerical error.

139 words; within budget

At time t, prediction sets include responses whose scores are at or below the 1 - alpha_t quantile. Once the response is revealed, adaptive conformal inference updates alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha - err_t), where alpha is the target miscoverage, gamma is positive and err_t indicates a miss. A miss lowers the level, relaxing the inclusion cutoff; a covered response reverses this adjustment. Summing the recursion shows that average miscoverage differs from alpha by (alpha_1 - alpha_{T+1})/(gamma T). Extending quantiles outside [0, 1] to produce full or empty sets keeps alpha_t between -gamma and 1 + gamma. Thus bounded parameter motion, rather than a correct model of the shifting distribution, makes the average discrepancy vanish as T grows. This operation controls accumulated coverage error, but its guarantee permits extreme sets and does not ensure accurate coverage in short windows.

Shared initial draft

Let alpha be the desired miscoverage frequency and alpha_t the level supplied to the score quantile at time t. After observing whether the prediction set misses the response, the algorithm updates alpha_{t+1} = alpha_t + gamma(alpha - err_t), where err_t is one for a miss and gamma is positive. Lowering alpha_t raises the quantile threshold, counteracting undercoverage; increasing it reverses that adjustment. The distribution-free analysis sums this recursion, expressing cumulative miscoverage error through the difference between the initial and final levels. Quantile conventions outside [0, 1] produce full or empty sets, keeping the levels bounded and making the average discrepancy vanish as the horizon grows. This operation controls accumulated coverage error without modelling the shift, but permits extreme sets and does not by itself guarantee accurate coverage in short windows.

P12 / Results / Sample 3

Adaptive Conformal Inference Under Distribution Shift

Baseline Primary: pass; text-only: pass

152 words; within budget

The volatility comparisons distinguish the benefit of adaptive calibration from the effect of score design. With normalized errors and step size 0.005, adaptive coverage in 500-day windows stayed near the 90% target, with fluctuations comparable to the plotted Bernoulli reference. Fixed-level calibration showed larger departures, including Fannie Mae's undercoverage during the 2008 crisis. Both methods used rolling 1,250-day GARCH fits and score quantiles, so refitting alone did not prevent the fixed method's failure. Switching to unnormalized absolute errors produced much wider adaptive coverage swings, showing that feedback did not remove sensitivity to score design. The four displayed stocks were selected from twelve for conspicuous fixed-method failures. In the county-vote experiment, time-zone ordering with randomization within zones yielded fixed-method troughs around zone changes, whereas adaptive calibration maintained approximately 90% local coverage in 300-county windows. These comparisons support local correction under the tested drift constructions, not guaranteed pointwise coverage or shorter intervals.
